## PART ONE.

L’AUTHOR of this writing is a French Protestant <term>French Protestant: Often referred to as Huguenots, these individuals were frequently forced to write anonymously or from exile due to religious persecution in 17th and 18th-century France.</term>, whose name does not need to appear, and who even wishes that no one inquire after it for reasons which can easily be judged by reading the Work that I am presenting to the public. It begins with a sort of addition to the reflections that the Author of the Book under examination has

### Plan of this Work.

of having acted thus, the Court, after having destroyed several individual Churches one after the other, ventured to attack the entire body of all the Churches of a large Province, by sending the Dragoons <term>Dragoons: Cavalry soldiers (Dragons) used by Louis XIV to harass Protestant households into converting to Catholicism, a brutal practice known as the "dragonnades."</term> to Poitou <term>Poitou: A region in western France that was a stronghold of the Huguenot population and the site of the first dragonnades in 1681.</term>.

It is shown next how the Court, having noticed by this trial run that it was easy to oppress all the Reformed <note>original: "Reformés"</note> of the Kingdom by the most violent means, without fearing any unrest <note>original: "émotion," referring here to a popular uprising or public outcry</note> on their part, thought only of taking some measures to prevent the disadvantageous effect that the persecution might have on foreign Protestants. One speaks on this subject of the measures it took for this purpose, and the efforts that the Ministers and Agents of France made to portray the persecution as a chimera <term>Chimera: A fabrication or illusion; the French government officially denied that any violent persecution was taking place to avoid international backlash.</term>, at the very time when it was already quite cruel,

## SECOND PART.

After these first reflections, the Examination of the *Apology for the Pastors* <term>Apology for the Pastors: A defense or formal justification (from the Greek "apologia") written to vindicate the conduct of Protestant ministers during the religious persecutions in France.</term> begins; and as we agree on several points with the Author of this *Apology*, we shall focus particularly only on those points upon which we hold an opposing view to his—namely, all those arguments that tend to persuade the reader that the Pastors did not fail in their duty <note>original: "manqué à leur devoir"</note>

## Plan of this Work.

It is first shown that although the persecutors are wrong to blame the departure of the Pastors—they being the cause of it through their cruelties—it does not follow that the Reformed <note>The Huguenots, or French Protestants</note> cannot legitimately complain about it; even those who have fallen <term>fallen: Referring to the "lapsi," those who renounced their Protestant faith under the pressure of persecution, often referred to at the time as "New Catholics."</term>, who have all the more reason to do so as their fall has rendered them more miserable; although this departure cannot fundamentally serve as a legitimate excuse for them. one cannot put these in parallel with the other; and it is shown, as much by the conduct of St. Athanasius as by several things he says himself in his **Apology**, that this Father would have in no way approved of a retreat <note>In this context, "retreat" refers to the act of pastors leaving their congregations to escape persecution.</note> such as the one in question here. # Plan of this Work.

since St. Augustine <note>Saint Augustine of Hippo (354–430 AD), a Bishop in North Africa whose writings on the duties of clergy during the Vandal invasions were central to this 17th-century debate.</note> condemned the flight of **Pastors** in the case that was proposed to him, with even greater reason would he have condemned it in the case currently under discussion; regarding the second [remark], that as this Father knew of persecution only by hearsay, his opinion ought to carry even more weight; and regarding the third, that the sentiments he held concerning the necessity of the **Ministry** <term>Ministry: The formal religious office and the pastoral duties associated with serving a congregation.</term> do not prevent his reasons from having great force in relation to the sentiments we hold regarding it; for these reasons, we refute everything the Author of the **Apology** <note>The "Author of the Apology" likely refers to a contemporary writer (such as Pierre Jurieu) who argued that Protestant ministers were justified in fleeing France after the Revocation of the Edict of Nantes in 1685.</note> says from page 200 to page 210. ## Plan of this Work.

Here we explain the passage from Matthew 10 <note>Matthew 10:23. This verse was a central point of contention between those who argued that clergy should stay with their flocks during persecution and those who argued they were permitted to flee.</note>: *If they persecute you in one city, flee into another;* and we demonstrate that this command concerned only the first Disciples of Jesus Christ whom this Divine Savior sent into the cities of Judea; we show that it is not this command alone which authorizes the flight of the faithful in particular, and that there are several other reasons and authorities that render it legitimate.

We demonstrate the difference between the Ministry of the **Apostles** and that of **Pastors** <term>Pastors: Here referring to settled ministers of a specific local congregation, as opposed to the itinerant, universal mission of the Apostles.</term> attached to a particular Church; we show thereby that the flight of the former provides no precedent for authorizing that of the latter, and we examine everything the Author of the **Apology** <note>Likely referring to Pierre Jurieu, a prominent Protestant leader who defended the flight of Huguenot ministers following the Revocation of the Edict of Nantes in 1685.</note> says on this subject from page 254 to page 261.

<meta>This page begins the "Avertissement" or Notice to the Reader. It follows the general plan mentioned in the previous section, where the author justifies his decision to critique the Protestant ministers who fled France during the religious persecutions of the late 17th century.</meta>

# NOTICE.

<image-desc>A decorative drop cap 'O' set within an oval frame, surrounded by fruit and leaf motifs.</image-desc>

ONE might perhaps find it strange that I am writing today against afflicted **Pastors** <term>Pastors: In this context, these are the Protestant (Huguenot) ministers in France facing persecution under the reign of Louis XIV.</term> who seem to have more need of consolation than of reproaches: but I hope that once this writing has been read, it will be clearly seen that I had no intention of increasing the bitterness of their affliction, nor of doing anything that goes against the rules of charity. I would never have written against their **withdrawal** <note>This refers to the "Retraite," the flight or exile of Protestant ministers from France following the Revocation of the Edict of Nantes (1685), which made their ministry illegal.</note>, if no one had taken it upon themselves to write a **Defense** <note>original: "Apologie"; here meaning a formal written justification of their actions.</note> of it; and I even had quite a lot of difficulty in resolving to do so after having seen the one that was brought to light some time ago; because I always feared that my sentiments might do some harm to the **Pastors**, whose conduct in this regard I cannot help but disapprove. However, after having examined things a little more closely, I determined both to publish my sentiments on this subject, and to refute those of the Author of that **Defense** which are contrary to them: as much because I hoped that my work might serve in some way the advancement of the glory of God, as because I believed myself obliged to prevent, with all my power, two bad effects that the book I am examining might produce.

One will see clearly enough by the reading of this work how and in what way it can serve the advancement of the glory of God, and the edification of the Church, without my focusing here on pointing it out. As for the two bad effects that the **Defense**

<meta>This page continues the "Notice to the Reader" (Avertissement). The previous page ended with the partial word "gl...", likely intended to be "gloire" (glory) or part of a phrase discussing the "Apologie." [Apolo]gy of the **Pastors** could produce, one will doubtless see as well as I do, that they are too considerable to be neglected, and that it is very necessary that they be prevented. For first, if the sentiments that I refute were received among us without any contradiction, all the enemies of our **Religion**, and principally those of **Rome** <term>Rome: A standard 17th-century shorthand for the Roman Catholic Church and the Papacy.</term>, would not fail to impute them, according to their custom, to the whole body of the **Reformed** <term>Reformed: Refers to the French Protestant (Huguenot) church following the theology of John Calvin.</term>; and to accuse us of having sentiments too relaxed regarding the **duties** of **Pastors**, and regarding the engagements of the **Holy Ministry**. And secondly, it might also happen in the future that other **Pastors**, finding themselves in similar circumstances, would believe they could legitimately abandon their **Flocks**, following the example of our **Pastors**, supported and confirmed by the pretended reasons of the Author of this **Defense**.

I strive to prevent these two bad effects by this writing: first, by taking away from the enemies of our **Religion** every pretext to accuse us of laxity on this subject, by showing that the sentiments of the Author of the **Defense** are only the sentiments of a private individual which can establish no precedent, and by showing that our best Authors are directly opposed to him in all things where one might accuse him of having sentiments a bit too relaxed. And secondly, by destroying with all my power the impressions that might be made in the future by the bad example of the **Pastors** who have departed from **France**, and the pretended reasons used to make their **Defense**.

Since I was not among the first who saw the Book that I examine; and as I have been otherwise occupied from time to time with other things, it is not surprising that this response does not appear as soon as it

<meta>This page continues the author’s opening argument. Having previously mentioned that the pastors left their flocks following the King's Edict, the author now characterizes this "retreat" as a failure of duty, transitioning from private disagreement to a public refutation of those who defended the ministers' flight.</meta>

<header>Disinterested Sentiments on the</header>
<meta>catchword: dir</meta>

their care, was the fatal consequence of their departure, I have always regarded their retreat as a kind of desertion, which stains their Ministry with a mark they will find great difficulty washing away. Nevertheless, until now, I believed the rules of charity <term>charity: In a 17th-century theological context, this refers to 'caritas'—the Christian obligation to act out of love and avoid harsh judgment of others' failings.</term> obliged me to conceal this feeling, knowing well that they sinned out of weakness rather than malice; and that their failing did not render them unworthy of the protection and aid they found in the places of their exile. I would remain silent still, had they kept silent themselves, and if an Apology <term>Apology: A formal written defense or justification of one's conduct (from the Greek 'apologia'), rather than an admission of guilt.</term> for their retreat had not appeared some time ago, which seeks to place their actions entirely beyond reproach. I was among the last to see this Apology, as it only came into my hands long after it had been made public; but I have perhaps been one of those who read it with the greatest care; and since my views do not entirely align with those of its author <note>The author refers to Isaac Maurin, who wrote the "Histoire & Apologie de la Retraite des Pasteurs" (1687).</note>, I confess that this reading has determined me to publish them; so that they might produce the intended effect for the glory of God, and for the edification of His Church.

I might have done so previously in a few words and in a simple letter; however, I can no longer confine myself presently to such narrow limits; for to properly establish my sentiments, I shall be obliged to refute those of the author of this Apology which are opposed to them.

I would still refrain from making them public, if I believed they could harm the persecuted Pastors, or cool—

I therefore publish my sentiments on the retreat of the Pastors, in the hope that they will produce no effect contrary to my intentions—which, far from tending to cool the charity of the people, serve only to increase it if possible; and above all to awaken the zeal of all those who desire the advancement of the kingdom of JESUS CHRIST, and who are committed to working toward the restoration of the Churches of France <note>This refers to the Reformed (Huguenot) churches, which were dismantled by the Revocation of the Edict of Nantes in 1685.</note>.

I wish with all my heart that our views might unite to align with this same goal: and that the subject I must treat does not suffer the same fate as so many others that are daily the cause of ordinary disputes; where it seems people fight more out of a sense of pride <note>original: "point d’honneur," referring to a competitive desire to maintain one's reputation or "save face" in an argument.</note> than from a love of the truth, which might be of some use for the advancement of the glory of God.

->Retreat of the Pastors of France<-

The treatise on the **Retreat of the Pastors** <note>In this context, "retreat" refers to the flight or exile of Protestant ministers from France to neighboring countries to escape persecution.</note> that I intend to examine briefly here consists of three parts. The first contains an **abridged history** of the various means used to destroy the **Reformed party** <note>The "Reformed" refers to the Huguenots, French Protestants who followed the teachings of John Calvin.</note> in France, from the **Reformation** until the **revocation of the Edict of Nantes**, by which their ruin was finally completed. The second contains several reasons to justify the retreat of the Pastors, against those who criticize it: specifically those who, having succumbed in France, claim that this retreat can serve as an excuse for their own failure <note>The author is referring to "nouveaux convertis" (new converts)—Protestants who converted to Catholicism under duress and then blamed the fleeing pastors for abandoning their flocks.</note>. And the third is a continuation of these same reasons, regarding those who have not fallen and who still suffer for the truth: or those who, being outside the Kingdom, had no personal part in the persecution.

I will focus primarily on the examination of these last two parts, where my views are, in several respects, directly opposed to those of the Author of this work. As for the first part, everything he reports there is based on acts so authentic, or of such public notoriety, that one would have to be a paid agent <term>pensionnaire: Someone receiving a stipend or salary, implying the person is bribed by the Catholic Church to defend its actions.</term> of the **Clergy of France**, and have neither good faith nor shame, to contradict him. His conjectures also seem to me very well-drawn; and his reflections so just, that I wholeheartedly agree <note>original: "donne de bon cœur les mains," an idiom meaning to offer one's hand in agreement or to yield to an argument.</note> with everything he has put forward in this first part of his work. I will therefore content myself in this regard by adding a few remarks to his own, in order to confirm the truth of what he has stated, and to follow a method in this work entirely consistent

He very rightly noted on pages 29 and 30 the primary cause of our misfortunes, attributing it to the policy of those who, in order to detach the Pastors from the common cause, allowed them to enjoy their privileges; while they gradually stripped the Churches, knowing well, according to the maxim of the Devil, <margin>Job 2:4</margin> that *skin for skin, yea, all that a man hath will he give for his life.* <note>original: "chacun donnera peau pour peau, & tout ce qu’il a pour ſa vie." This is a reference to the Book of Job, where Satan suggests to God that a person will endure any loss as long as their own physical life is spared.</note>

This hellish policy has undoubtedly succeeded only too well for those who put it into practice against us to destroy us; and it can be said that they owe to it all the success of their enterprises. However, it must be admitted that not all Pastors fell into the trap <term>donné dans le panneau: A French idiom meaning to be easily deceived or to fall into a snare.</term> so easily. There were some who, on certain occasions, gave signs of a true zeal; and who sacrificed their interests, and those of their families, to the public interest. But as the others were, beyond comparison, in much greater number, they <note>meaning the courageous pastors</note> had the misfortune of exposing themselves not only to all the mistreatment of our enemies: but also to the mockery of those who followed maxims directly opposed to theirs; and who did not fail to paint them as indiscreet zealots. As a result, this consideration prevented several from doing their duty when needed, as they would have done had they not feared being blamed by their own brothers; or if they could have hoped to be supported by them in their good resolutions.

to the Pastor. The Church was punished by being suddenly stripped of several of its most significant privileges: it was condemned to heavy fines for the benefit of certain communities of Monks and Nuns: individuals were punished through quartering <term>quartering: Known as "dragonnades," this was the forced housing of royal troops in Protestant homes to intimidate and financially ruin families until they converted to Catholicism.</term>, through proscriptions, and through banishments. There were even two unfortunate souls who were hanged as an example; and as for the Pastor, a criminal trial was brought against him shortly after based on false testimony, which brought him within two inches of the gallows: from which he was saved by a kind of miracle, after most of his Judges had voted for death.

As men usually judge matters only by their outcome, the conduct of this Church was condemned by all the others, who had chosen the path of obedience. It was forced to bear the burden of the punishments inflicted upon it all alone: to pay all the fines, without any other church willing to partially relieve its load. And as for the Pastor, besides what he had to endure from his enemies, he also had to bear the blame that usually follows advice which results in failure. This made it clear from then on that to destroy all the Churches of the Kingdom, one only had to attack them one after another, now in one Province and now in another, without fearing that they would ever be in a position to give one another the mutual aid they owed each other. It is true that the matter then became difficult, and even impossible later on, due to the mistake made in allowing the holding of Sy- <note>The text cuts off mid-word, continuing as "Synods" on the next page. These were the formal representative assemblies of the Reformed Church.</note>

National Synods; which was a privilege upon which the preservation of the Churches depended; and the only means we had to be able to make remonstrances <note>Formal protests or petitions addressed to the King.</note> in the name of all the Reformed <term>Reformed: Refers to the Huguenots, French Protestants following the tradition of John Calvin.</term> of the Kingdom. For although we had, until the very end, a General Deputy <term>General Deputy: An official representative meant to advocate for Protestant interests at the royal court.</term> near the King, it had been a very long time since the Churches had nominated him; instead, it was the King himself who chose him for his post, who gave him his pension, and upon whom he absolutely depended. In such a way, it was more accurate to say that he was the King’s man—meant to lull the Churches to sleep, to put them off with fine words, and to prevent them from complaining—rather than the General Deputy of the Reformed Churches of the Kingdom, meant to secure their well-being and to oppose the injustices and vexations for which they had, every day, so many just grounds to complain.

   Things being in this fatal state, the Court was content for eighteen or twenty years to strip us of our privileges little by little, and to attack now one Church, then another; without those that were not being attacked ever daring to join those being oppressed, to complain all at once about injustices in which they all had such a considerable interest. The Court then believed that, to more promptly complete our ruin, it was necessary to attack an entire Province all at once, and to see if the others would show any resentment, and if they would put themselves in a position to prevent their ruin through a firm resolution. If all the Churches of the Kingdom had united, at least at that time, when only a generous despair <note>A term for the collective courage that arises when a group realizes they have nothing left to lose but their faith and lives.</note> could have saved them from total ruin, perhaps they would have still made those who had been oppressing them for so long tremble. But they treated this oppressed Province in the same way they had treated individual Churches. Each one stayed at home, saying nothing, waiting to be devoured in turn; and the Court, after this fine trial run, waited only for a favorable circumstance to complete what it had so successfully begun.

It is not that the Court then believed it could complete our ruin all at once, as it has done since; although it moved very quickly, this work required several more years. For although we were already regarded from then on as nothing more than victims who could be sacrificed day by day without any resistance, there were still several diplomatic considerations to maintain with foreign Protestants, who did not view this persecution with a favorable eye. 

England still had a King at that time who made a profession of being Protestant <note>Referring to Charles II, who was publicly Protestant but privately favored Catholicism and received subsidies from the French King Louis XIV.</note>; and at a time when we were much less oppressed, the Parliament had taken offense at the injustices beginning to be done to us—indeed, in a manner that made it clear they took a great interest in our fate. ...testants <note>The word "Protestants" begins on the previous page as "Prote-" and concludes here.</note> had also shown, either by their intercessions or by the difficulties that our interests sometimes brought to the Negotiations, that our ruin was not a matter of indifference to them. Thus, it was still necessary to keep up some appearances with them: and to make them believe, as much as was possible, that the persecution was not such as the persecuted represented it in their places of refuge <note>original: "retraite"</note>.

This was a difficult task at a time when one already saw, throughout almost all of Europe, an infinite number of wretches who had been forced to leave everything because of the persecution they had feared, or that they had effectively suffered; especially since they had treated the Dauphiné and the Vivarais <note>Dauphiné and Vivarais were provinces in southeastern France. But when one possesses a great store of impudence, and when the people one deals with are quite willing to be deceived, one could make it believe that it is broad daylight even when night covers the earth with its thickest shadows.

France, as everyone knows, has skillful Ministers <note>Here, "Ministers" refers to diplomats or ambassadors representing the French Crown abroad.</note> everywhere, who create supporters for her among all the Princes and in all the States of Europe. These Ministers usually cut a very impressive figure in the places where they reside: they keep an open table; they feast well; and, besides a large number of Servants, they also have several types of men for every occasion, who serve them for various purposes according to their diverse talents. It is by means of these people that they learn much news of what is happening for or against the interests of their Master: it is through them that they sometimes know the hearts and intentions of the people who share the least: and it is also through them that they persuade, or that they dissuade, everything they wish to persuade or dissuade. They for a very long time absolutely denied that the <term>Reformed</term> <note>The "Reformés" refers to French Protestants (Huguenots), who followed the Reformed tradition of Christianity.</note> were being persecuted in any way; even when most of their religious services <note>original: "exercices"—specifically referring to the public practice of Protestant worship.</note> had already been taken away, as well as almost all their privileges. They portrayed the first persecuted people who left the Kingdom as people who had only left because of their crimes, or because of the disorder of their affairs: and it is only since the revocation of the <term>Edict of Nantes</term> <note>The 1598 decree that had provided French Protestants with religious and civil liberties; its revocation in 1685 led to intense state violence and mass exodus.</note>, and since the extreme violences that accompanied it, or that followed it, that they were forced to admit to the persecution. Until then they had always treated it as a fantasy <note>original: "chimere"—literally a "chimera," meaning a baseless or fabricated story.</note>, with an impudence that has no equal. They gave the lie to all those who had been witnesses...

<meta>catchword: land,</meta>

[persecu]tion were so harsh, and at the same time so striking, that by making even the impudence of those who until then had treated it as a fable and a fantasy <note>Original: "chimere." The author refers to how French diplomats previously dismissed reports of Protestant suffering as mere rumors or exaggerations.</note> blush, it could also move the pity of all good people; and lead them to make extraordinary efforts of charity to relieve this great number of faithful, whom this horrible persecution has reduced to the most extreme misery.

<margin>9</margin>
In effect, as soon as our persecutors saw on the throne of England a King of their own religion <note>James II of England, a Catholic who ascended the throne in February 1685. His reign was seen as a major blow to the international Protestant cause.</note>, who—after having quelled in just a few months all the unrest within his Kingdom <note>A reference to the Monmouth Rebellion of 1685, which James II suppressed, temporarily solidifying his power.</note>—was in a position to prevent his subjects from expressing any resentment for the evils inflicted upon us, they believed they should no longer delay the final blow with which they had already so often threatened us. Until then, the English had been the most formidable Protestants, and the most feared by those who might demand an accounting for the injustices done to us, and for those being done to us every day; they were primarily the ones whose very name still restrained, to some degree, the fury of our enemies. But as soon as they were subjected to a King of a religion opposite to their own, they were viewed only as people with their arms tied, from whom there was nothing left to fear. This, in my opinion, is what hastened the timing of our ruin, and why they completed all at once what they had initially intended to do in several stages. I believe I am well-justified in this thought because, as everyone knows, only a few months passed between the end of the unrest in England...

These are the remarks I felt duty-bound to add to those made by the Author of the *Apology* <note>In the 17th century, an "apology" was a formal written defense of a person's conduct or a religious doctrine, not an admission of guilt.</note> in the first part of his work. I could have accompanied them with the account of several other important facts, with rather specific circumstances; but that would have led me too far from the principal goal I set for myself in this work. I return to it now by moving to the second part of the book I am examining, where the Author begins to provide the *Apology* for the retreat of the Pastors who left France because of the persecution, after having provided the abridged History of that retreat in the first part of his work.

# E X A M I N A T I O N
### OF THE
# SECOND PART.

THERE are many things in this second part on which I am entirely of the Author's opinion; there are others on which I am only partially so; and finally others on which my opinion is entirely different from his. I will explain myself regarding these in the following pages as they present themselves, following the order that the Author himself has followed. After having finished his first part with a reflection he makes on the malice of the persecutors—who have driven the <term>Pastors</term> <note>In this context, "Pastors" refers to the Protestant ministers of the Reformed Church in France who were forced into exile or faced execution.</note> from the Kingdom and who try to make them odious to their Flocks because of their flight—he begins the second with another reflection on the conduct of the <term>Reformed</term> <note>The "Reformed" refers to the Huguenots, the French Protestants who followed the theological tradition of John Calvin.</note> who also blame their retreat, and who are quite glad to make it serve as an excuse for their own weakness <note>This "weakness" refers to those Protestants who chose to renounce their faith and convert to Catholicism (the "New Converts") to avoid persecution, often blaming their ministers' departure for their lack of spiritual resolve.</note>.

It is certain that the former show very poor grace in reproaching the Pastors for a flight that is but the effect of their own cruelty and injustice. And the Pastors may well say of them with reason what <term>St. Athanasius</term> <note>Athanasius of Alexandria (c. 296–373), a Church Father who was famously exiled multiple times and wrote a "Defense of His Flight" to justify his escapes from his theological and political enemies.</note> said of his persecutors: *They reproach us for having had the cowardice to flee persecution, and they do not notice that they reveal their cruelty through this reproach. For if it is an evil to flee persecution, it is indeed a much greater evil* <note>original: "Ils nous reprochent d'avoir eu la lâcheté de fuir la persecution, & ils ne prennent pas garde qu'ils decouvrent leur cruauté par ce reproche. Car si c'est un mal de fuir la persecution, c'est bien un plus grand mal..."</note>

of doing so; they should then be ashamed of the persecution they inflict upon us, instead of reproaching us for our flight as if it were a crime: let them cease to persecute us, and we will cease to flee..... Our flight is proof of their cruelty. One does not flee from good people; one only flees from the wicked and the cruel. All these reproaches fall quite justly, as everyone can see, upon the persecutors who today have the impudence to blame the flight of the <term>Pastors</term> <note>Protestant ministers.</note>, after having terrified them themselves with the horror of the punishments they had prepared for them, and by which they constrained them to obey the <term>Edict</term> <note>The Edict of Fontainebleau (1685), which revoked the Edict of Nantes and ordered all Protestant ministers to leave France within fifteen days or face the galleys or death.</note> that exiled them all with such injustice.

Not only can one not make any of these reproaches against them, but I believe that, at heart, they may justly complain of the conduct of their Pastors, who abandoned them entirely in the heat of the struggle: and at the time when they had the greatest need to be supported—either by their public and private exhortations, while that was still possible, or by secret counsel, when they could no longer help them in any other way.

They undoubtedly know well that their <term>Apostasy</term> <note>The formal abandonment of their Protestant faith.</note> is no less criminal, nor less condemnable; but it is to show that they are not the only guilty ones; and that if their Pastors can reproach them for not having fulfilled the duties of their calling, they can also, for their part, reproach the Pastors for not having fulfilled the duties of their Ministry. There is nothing more common among men than to see reproaches rebuffed by other reproaches: it is a kind of defense that takes the place of reason, and which in some way consoles those who find themselves fully convicted of the crimes with which they are charged. There is nothing more disagreeable to them than the reprimands they have justly deserved; and there is also nothing more agreeable to them than being able, in turn, to be the censors of their censors, by showing that those who have rebuked them are not themselves irreproachable. ### Retreat of the Pastors of France.

...tenderness. I admit all these things; I was myself among the number of those who regarded the departure of the **Pastors** <note>original: "Pasteurs" — Protestant ministers, specifically Huguenots.</note> as a stroke from Heaven, as a marvel of God’s **Providence**; and who take comfort in the hope of which the Author speaks in the same place, that God has preserved them *to bring about, sooner or later, the rebirth in France of the Reformation that they sought to destroy there.* I also do not wish to strip the fugitive **Pastors** of the glory of their **Confessions** <note>A "Confessor" in this context is one who suffers for their religious faith without being killed, distinguished from a "Martyr."</note>, nor the glory of martyrdom from those who have constantly suffered in prisons and the various torments of body or mind to which many were exposed for the **Gospel** <note>original: "l'Evangile"</note>. God forbid: I myself witnessed the great charity shown to them in many places; and one could see from the beginning those efforts of Christian charity that all true faithful owe to one another. But all this does not prevent me from regarding the retreat of the **Pastors** as a kind of **desertion**, by which they failed in one of the most important duties of their Ministry; and the Author of their **Apology** <note>A formal defense or justification of a position.</note> will permit me to tell him that I see nothing in the sentiments held regarding the retreat of the **Pastors**, nor in everything that Christian charity or human compassion has done for them, that serves as an **Apology** for this retreat, nor that can justify the weakness that most of them displayed in it.

<meta>This page continues the author's nuanced argument regarding the French Protestant ministers. <header>Disinterested Sentiments on the</header>

It is sufficient here to show how one can reconcile thoughts that seem so opposed to the Author of the *Apology* <note>Likely referring to Pierre Jurieu’s *Apologie pour leurs Sérénissimes Majestés* (1689) or a similar defense of the Huguenot ministers.</note>.

To do this, one need only distinguish the duties of a simple believer from the duties of a Pastor; these are indeed two very different things, as our Author remarks himself in another place. One can very often fulfill the former and fail in the latter; and I believe that this is precisely what happened to the fugitive or exiled **Pastors** of France. Most of them fulfilled the duty of a simple believer and, in this regard, earned the title of **Confessors** <term>Confessors: Historically, those who maintained their Christian faith under pressure or torture but did not suffer the death of a martyr.</term>; and some even that of **Martyrs**. But they all failed in the duty of their Ministry by abandoning their **Flocks** <note>Their congregations left behind in France.</note> on the day of visitation <note>A biblical term used here to describe the period of intense persecution and trial under Louis XIV.</note>: it is in this respect that they can be regarded as timid and as deserters. Under the first idea, they undoubtedly deserve great praise; and although under the latter they cannot shield themselves from the blame of their timidity and desertion, it is certain nonetheless that one could not, without committing a crime, regard them as people deserving of our hatred or contempt: because charity commands us to bear with the faults of our brothers. Moreover, their example should teach us to tremble for ourselves; we have neither more strength nor more light: and we would have perhaps, and apparently, made the same mistake as they did if we had been put to the same test.

Everyone knows that the temptation was harsh and violent: it was undoubtedly difficult not to be frightened and dazed in such an occasion com-

<page-num>32</page-num>
<header>Disinterested Sentiments on the [Charity...]</header>
<meta>catchword: rité (completing "charité" or "vérité")</meta>

*...contributes to their retreat: and when they feel a reluctance toward it, they are exhorted, they are urged, and violence is done to them to force them to withdraw... And if the affection of the faithful in the first centuries for the preservation of their Leaders <note original: "Conducteurs">Meaning the bishops and elders of the early church.</note> was the natural effect of a truly Christian charity, shall it be said that the sorrow felt by some Christians today regarding the safety of their Pastors is a reasonable effect of an Evangelical equity?* <note original: "équité Evangelique">A term referring to the fairness and justice required by the teachings of the Gospel.</note>

I will say first, once and for all, regarding these final words of the Author I have just quoted, and regarding everything similar or approaching it in his **Apology**, that I do not believe that among all the **Reformed** <note>The French Protestants or Huguenots.</note> who complain of the retreat of the **Pastors**, or who regard it as a failure in the duties of their **Ministry**, there could be anyone to whom their safety gives sorrow; and who would not most heartily wish to be able to reconcile the safety of their persons with the duties of their **Ministry**. It is, in my opinion, to do them a very great wrong to attribute their complaints, or their sentiments, to such unreasonable motives, so little worthy indeed of an **Evangelical equity**.

After this small remark, I believe it is good that I make two or three more general ones on all that I have just cited. The first is that it is, in my opinion, a very suspicious method, and one very prone to falling into error, which attaches itself first to **prejudices** <note original: "prejugés">In this context, it refers to preconceived notions or popular opinions used as a primary argument.</note>. However legitimate they may seem to those who hold them, or to those who receive them, it is certain that one must always be very wary of them, and that they deceive more often than they serve to make the **truth** known.

<summary>The author argues that critics of the fleeing pastors do not begrudge them their safety, but rather wish that safety could coexist with their religious duties. He also warns that relying on popular sentiment (prejudice) is a flawed way to determine the truth of the matter.</summary>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 32, the author concludes a thought about "truth" and how it should guide one's actions. He is systematically dismantling the logical structure of an "Apology" (written by Pierre Jurieu) which was intended to justify why Protestant ministers fled France during the religious persecutions of the 1680s.</meta>

<page-num>33</page-num>

# Retreat of the Pastors of France.

[truth] <note original: "...rité">The text completes the word "verité" (truth) from the previous page.</note>, and to make the right side followed. We all know enough news of this, and we have seen convincing enough proofs of it, to oblige us to reject this method; to show that we seek the truth in good faith; and that we despise the ruses of those who wish to catch men by surprise, and dazzle them at first with appearances. 

My second remark is that **proofs of fact**, based on examples of what has happened in the past, are also very defective proofs; because, following the maxim of the **Logicians** <term>Logicians: Scholars of logic who established the rules for valid reasoning and argument.</term>, one cannot conclude anything from a specific fact to another. The slightest circumstance, more or less, strips away all the force from these types of proofs, as I will show in what follows when speaking of the examples that the Author cites to support his opinion. 

Finally, my third remark is that to bring the truth to light in all matters where the opinions of men are divided, one must always make **proofs of law** <term>proofs of law (French: "preuves de droit"): Arguments based on fundamental principles, theological mandates, or legal rights, rather than historical examples.</term> walk before **proofs of fact** and before prejudices; and use the latter only to confirm the truth once it has already been made evident by the former. The Author of the **Apology** <note>Referring to the published defense of the Huguenot pastors who fled France.</note> would perhaps have followed this method himself, if he had had another cause to defend, and if he had first felt the strength of the **proofs of law** that he wanted to use. But not having felt this strength himself—because, in fact, they do not have enough of it to make themselves felt—he believed that to make them more easily accepted, he had to lead with prejudices and examples.

He says, in speaking *of the ancient Christians*, who

For our Author has allowed himself to be so greatly deceived by his own prejudice that he believes he has placed beyond doubt the thing in the world that is most doubtful, to say nothing more forceful. 

If there were a formal opposition between the conduct of the ancient faithful and that of the Christians today of whom he speaks, it is certain that the one could not have earned praise without the other being exposed to blame. Thus I can overturn the proposition of our Author; and I am sure that it will be found incomparably more true by saying that although the conduct of the faithful of whom he speaks is praiseworthy, that does not prevent the reproach with which the **Pastors** are charged today from being legitimate.

# Retreat of the Pastors of France.

The Author then, without realizing it, makes the case against those for whom he has undertaken a defense <note>original: "Apologie," meaning a formal justification of a position or conduct</note>: for he adds at the same time that when the Leaders <note>original: "Conducteurs," referring to the spiritual guides of the early Christian church</note> of whom he speaks showed reluctance—that is, toward retreating or fleeing—people exhorted them, pressured them, and used force *to compel them to withdraw.* I find in all of this no argument <note>original: "prejugé," which in this context means a preceding example or legal grounds for an argument</note> in favor of the Pastors against the Flocks <term>Flocks: The congregations of the Reformed (Protestant) Church in France.</term> who complain about the retreat of their ministers. These modern ministers fled persecution without showing any reluctance to the Flocks they were abandoning, and without waiting for the faithful to have exhorted or pressured them, or for force to have been used upon them.

From where could this great reluctance, which the ancient Leaders of the Church showed toward leaving their Flocks to seek shelter from persecution, have come? Certainly, I see no good reason for it, if not for the fear of failing in the duties of their Ministry; and I believe I would be doing an injustice to these illustrious figures if I attributed their resistance on these occasions to some other motive less worthy of them. It is easy enough to see how this creates a case against the fugitive or exiled Pastors of our time, without me explaining it further here. I come now to the examples our Author adds to strengthen his alleged arguments, as so many proofs of fact.

The faithful of France are currently under the Cross <note>A common 17th-century Protestant expression for living under persecution and suffering for one's faith.</note>, and they long for the return of their Pastors so that the Gospel might be preached to them while they are under the Cross. I find this desire very holy and very legitimate, and I believe it to be shared by the most virtuous of people. It is true that the Pastors could not return to France and preach there under the Cross without being daily exposed to the danger of the Galleys <note>The "Galleys" refers to the *Galères*, a brutal form of forced labor as a rower on naval ships, a common sentence for Huguenot men.</note> and the gallows; this is what makes them look upon the desire of those who wish for their return as a voice calling them to the gallows or the Galleys. They are wrong in this: to judge it sanely and fairly, one must attribute all these things to the misfortune of the times, and in no way to the intention of those who are suffering themselves, and who only desire the return of their Pastors to receive from them the instructions and consolations that are so necessary to them in the midst of so many struggles and so many sufferings.

# Withdrawal of the Pastors of France

...[that] the stay of the Pastors in France was impossible; and yet the interest of <term>public edification <gloss>The spiritual growth and communal strengthening of the faithful.</gloss></term> made it necessary. I shall state the reasons for this in what follows; however, since, following the maxim of our Author, one must avoid the final rigors of persecution only when public edification is not at stake, it is evident that the Pastors were obliged to remain in France despite the <note>The Edict of Fontainebleau (1685), which revoked the Edict of Nantes, ordered all Protestant ministers to leave France within 15 days or face the galleys.</note> Edict which ordered them to leave. They also ought to have made every effort of which they were capable to prevent the fall of their **Flocks** <note>A common metaphor for the Protestant congregations.</note>, while they were still standing, or to raise them up once they had fallen, without regard for the dangers to which they remained exposed, except as much as Christian prudence might allow.

<margin>Page 93.</margin>
But "what could they do in this cruel situation?" says the Author of the *Apology*, speaking of all these difficulties. I reply to this that they could have disguised themselves, for example, by wearing secular clothes; coming and going during the day in places where they could not be recognized; returning at night, when it was possible, and showing themselves to some of the faithful who were best known for their piety; exhorting them to their duty; writing to them when it was not possible to speak to them; making known to them by all the means they could devise that they had not abandoned them; in a word, making every effort on their part and fulfilling all the duty of good Pastors, and committing the rest to the care and guidance of **Providence**. I admit that all these precautions were not enough to put them out of danger; and I do not doubt

Furthermore, since the Author of the *Apology* himself describes this distressing situation to us

<margin>P. 92.</margin>
as *a situation similar to that of a general rout after a great battle*; he will not take it poorly if I point out here that, just as in those kinds of situations good **Officers** are obliged to rally their soldiers, to encourage them, to lead them back to the fight, to manage an honorable retreat, or to die at their post, the Pastors—who are obliged to do for their **Flocks** everything that a brave **Captain** is obliged to do for his soldiers—ought to have acted in much the same way in this similar situation. I even believe that the **Flocks** have more reason to complain of the desertion of their **Pastors**, than the **Pastors** have to impute to their **Flocks** the entire fault of their fall, and to believe themselves beyond reproach.

I have something to say about both the first and the second responses. Regarding the first, I say that the Author is greatly mistaken in his thought that the **Churches** did not leave their **Pastors** the leisure to set good examples for them, because of that sudden fall and that general <term>revolt <gloss>Original: "revolte." The narrator uses this term to describe the mass conversion of Protestants to Catholicism under state pressure, viewing it as a betrayal of their faith.</gloss></term> which can be seen as the disgrace of our time and the great scandal of all truly faithful people. He intends, in my opinion, to suggest by this that good examples of constancy and firmness would have been useless to those who yielded after their revolt; and he consequently supposes, as far as I can understand his thought, that it was no longer necessary or possible to set good examples for them after their fall. For, without that, I cannot understand what he says: that *the Churches did not leave their Pastors the leisure to set good examples for them.* This is a supposition that has undoubtedly misled him; for it is certain that the sudden fall of the **Churches**, far from making good examples of firmness and constancy useless, made them on the contrary doubly necessary—not only to support those who were still standing, but also and primarily to lift up those who had so cowardly and unfortunately fallen <note>The narrator is arguing that even if a congregation converted to Catholicism under duress, the pastor's duty was to stay and encourage them to return to their original faith.</note>. Consequently, it is incomparably truer to say that the **Churches**, far from failing to leave the **Pastors** the leisure to set good examples by their sudden fall, actually pressured them to provide them; because this fall should have been a new motive to compel them to give these much-needed examples.

<margin>P. 99.</margin>

<header>Disinterested Sentiments on the [Retreat of the Pastors of France]</header>

<margin>In the same place.</margin>
In what place, and at what time, should a Pastor have set the good example that it is claimed he owed to his **Flock**? I answer this in two words: it was at the time and in the place of the struggle; on the day and on the field of battle. It was necessary that each Pastor remain not only within the **Kingdom** <note>The Kingdom of France, during the persecutions following the Revocation of the Edict of Nantes in 1685.</note>, but as close to his **Flock** as was possible. It was necessary that, no longer being able to make his voice heard above the noise of this storm, he make it known, as far as possible, that he was not far off, and that he always held himself ready to return. It was necessary that, no longer being able to gather his **Flock** in a temple <note>Protestant places of worship in France were specifically called "temples" to distinguish them from Catholic "churches."</note> or in the public place of their accustomed religious exercises, he should seek out for himself cellars, attics, caves, forests, or some other secret place to begin holy assemblies anew, and to preach the **Gospel** to all those who would have had the courage to follow him. In a word, it was necessary to employ every means to try to rebuild what the **Demon** <note>original: "le Demon." The author uses this term to personify the forces of persecution or the spiritual enemy of the church.</note> had just destroyed, after having made every effort to prevent that destruction. If, while working on such a holy task, he had fallen into the hands of the **persecutors**, he should have given glory to God through his <term>Confession <gloss>A public declaration of faith, often made under the threat of death or imprisonment.</gloss></term> and through **Martyrdom**, crowning all these noble labors with examples of constancy and firmness. These examples, with the grace of the **Holy Spirit**, might have confounded the persecutors and encouraged toward their duty all those who had been spectators of their struggles and witnesses to their victory.

Here the Apology <note>A formal written defense, specifically referring to the published justifications for the pastors' exile.</note> [says], *see changing etc.* It is not a strange thing, and there is less cause to be astonished by the treasons of these *base souls* than to be saddened that people *of such shameful character* had been admitted to a post as honorable as that of Captain. However, the number of these traitors *was small* in comparison to that of the *zealous Captains who preferred the honor of their duties, and the loyalty they owed to their Republic, to all the comforts of life.* But what number of brave Captains can be opposed to this small number of cowards? What number of faithful Officers who, on such and such an occasion, had exposed themselves to such and such a danger, had suffered such and such fatigue, had received such and such a wound, had been for such and such a time in the enemy's prisons, etc.; who even while leaving the battlefield on this last occasion, and making the retreat of which their soldiers complain and which others blame, suffered the grief of abandoning fathers and mothers, children, brothers, sisters, relatives, friends, property, and inheritances.

Certainly, if the case I am imagining had effectively happened, and if an Apologist used these kinds of arguments, I do not believe there would be anyone who would not immediately see their weakness; and who would not be capable of replying in two words: it is not a question now of what the Captains had done before the day of battle; what they did—or rather, what they failed to do—on that day is the subject of the blame and the complaints that one must address to properly defend them. They are blamed, and people complain, because they obeyed the orders of

the enemy, and abandoned their soldiers on the day of battle. It is precisely a matter of justifying this action, if it were possible, and not at all of exalting the value of the others. For, fundamentally, in affairs of this nature, it almost always happens that the final actions of men, when they are bad, tarnish the brilliance of the good actions they had performed previously; but it never happens that the first actions can remove the blame from the last, nor prevent the just reproaches that one can make against those who have committed them.

I reply then to the Author of the *Apology for the Retreat of the Pastors*, <note>This refers to a specific contemporary text defending the ministers who fled France. In the 17th century, an "Apology" was a formal legal or theological defense of one's conduct.</note> what one might reply to the supposed Apologist for the desertion of the Captains of whom I have just spoken. Thus, the second response of our Author does not destroy the objection he had made himself, because it does not address the facts. For although the authority that exiled the Pastors from the Kingdom was that to which they ought to be subject, and which reigned legitimately over them regarding **temporal** <term>Temporal: Relating to worldly, civil, or secular matters, as opposed to spiritual or eternal ones.</term> matters; it was nevertheless the declared and recognized enemy of their **Ministry**, <note>The spiritual office and responsibilities of the clergy.</note> and of everything regarding **spiritual** matters. <margin>Matthew 22:21</margin>
Regarding what Scripture commands us concerning the obedience due to the higher powers, it is well known that Scripture itself has very precisely marked for us the limits that must be placed on this obedience. Jesus Christ, in teaching us that we must *render unto Caesar <note>original: "Cesar"; a reference to the secular authorities, specifically King Louis XIV in this context.</note> what belongs to Caesar*, taught us at the same time that we must *render unto God what belongs to God*: and although, in practice, the first of these two duties is generally much more religiously observed than the other; it is nonetheless certain that the latter is incomparably more indispensable than the former, and that among all those who believe there truly is a God, there has never been dispute over the preference due to the latter of these duties, when one cannot reconcile them both together. I mean that when the orders of Caesar are contrary to the will of God, all those who believe there is a God agree that one must obey God and disobey Caesar.

# Disinterested Sentiments on the
# Kingdom. <note>The header is a continuation of the title from the previous page: "Disinterested Sentiments on the [Retreat of the Pastors of the] Kingdom."</note>

Finally, St. Paul, who is the one of all the Apostles who seems to carry this subjection the furthest, by
<margin>Rom. 13:5</margin>
saying that one must *be subject not only for [fear of] wrath, but also for conscience' sake*; shows quite evidently, at the same time, what are the things in which we
<margin>v. 7</margin>
must be subject to them, by adding: *Render therefore to all what is due to them: to whom tax, tax; to whom toll, toll; to whom fear, fear; to whom honor, honor*: to show that this subjection only concerns temporal, civil, or political matters. As soon as Sovereigns or Magistrates undertake to reign over the consciences of their subjects, they usurp a right that belongs only to God, who—being the only one who searches the hearts and minds <note>original: "fonde les cœurs & les reins"; literally "probes the hearts and kidneys," a biblical idiom for examining one's deepest thoughts and motives.</note> and who knows the thoughts of men—is also the only one who has the right to reign over our hearts and to regulate our thoughts according to his good pleasure.

It is to basely flatter the pride of Princes to persuade them that their power extends beyond these bounds; it is to attribute to men rights that belong only to God; and it is to open the door to tyranny. Reasonable and moderate Princes must be content with the power that God has given them and which legitimately belongs to them, without encroaching upon those rights which God has reserved for himself, and which are naturally beyond their reach. And it is quite certain that a truly wise Prince, fearing God, and capable of judging his rights without the prejudice of self-love and with just discernment, will always reject the advice of base flatterers who wish to lead him to

# Retreat of the Pastors of France.

...extend his power beyond these natural boundaries, which he could not cross without becoming a tyrant, and without making himself worthy of all the curses of men, and of all the wrath of God.

I am convinced that the Author of the *Apology* <note>Likely referring to a contemporary "Apologie pour les Pasteurs" (Apology for the Pastors), a genre of writing used by Huguenots to defend their conduct during the persecutions leading up to the Revocation of the Edict of Nantes.</note> is roughly of my sentiment in this regard; and that knowing well the limits of the power that one man can have over other men, and of the commands he has the right to give them, he also knows very well the limits of the dependence one must have toward him, and of the obedience that each person owes to human authority. But he will allow me to reproach the Pastors here, for when they were exiled, most had already, on several occasions and in several matters, carried this obedience beyond those legitimate bounds of which I have just spoken; and [they] disobeyed God to obey man. Thus, when they were forbidden to speak against the Roman Religion <note>Referring to the Roman Catholic Church, the state religion of France at the time.</note> under the pretext that it is the King's Religion, most of them no longer dared to give to its errors, its superstitions, and its idolatries the names that suit them so well, and which our blessed Reformers and even our fathers had used with such success to inspire in the people all the horror and aversion that one should justly have for this abominable Religion. That was undoubtedly one of the principal parts of the duty of their Ministry; and there was neither order, nor threat, nor corporal punishment that could excuse them from fulfilling it with all the zeal that must accompany the work of the Holy Ministry. However

<header>Disinterested Sentiments on the</header>

When this prohibition was imposed upon them, they believed it would be enough to teach the truth of the Gospel in the Temples <note>In 17th-century France, Protestant places of worship were legally required to be called "Temples" to distinguish them from Catholic "Églises" (Churches).</note>; and to maintain there the purest service of God, and that it was not necessary to use stinging—yet expressive and natural—terms, by which our fathers had been accustomed to decrying the errors, superstitions, and Roman idolatry. I do not even know if, after that, there did not also mingle in a bit of the delicacy of the age <note>A reference to the increasingly refined and "polite" social standards of 17th-century French high society, which looked down upon the blunt, confrontational rhetoric of the early Reformation.</note>, which made them regard these terms as harsh and coarse, no longer to the taste of the century, and which had earned our fathers the reputation of being fierce and intractable people. Whatever the case may be, I am firmly persuaded that they were much mistaken; and that by this conduct they allowed all the Churches of the Kingdom to fall gradually into this unhappy weakness, which caused them to succumb as soon as they were attacked with violence.

Indeed, if the people had always been maintained in that great aversion and in that great distance which they held toward the Communion of Rome—while being told every day that it is superstitious, idolatrous, Pagan, and abominable in all the things that separate us from it—it would undoubtedly have been much more difficult to persuade them to re-enter a Communion so detested; and they would have consequently been much more capable of putting up a vigorous resistance when the attempt was made to oblige them to commit this great crime <note>Referring to the forced conversions of Protestants to Catholicism, often under the threat of the "Dragonnades" (state-sponsored domestic terrorism).</note>. It is true that one might say to this that although these terms and their like had been removed from

<meta>This page continues the author’s argument that Protestant pastors made a strategic error by complying with royal restrictions on their speech. from public sermons and the most modern books of <term>Controversy</term> <note>Theological debates between Protestants and Catholics intended to prove the errors of the opposing side.</note>, they did not fail to skillfully insinuate the truth of the matter; and the people knew well enough what they ought to believe about it. But I answer to that, that these general ideas lost almost all their force once people no longer dared take the liberty of renewing them every day in public worship; especially at a time when it was more necessary than ever to give the most vivid and strongest impressions of them. This is because the <term>Papists</term> <note>A term used by 17th-century Protestants to refer to Roman Catholics, emphasizing their allegiance to the Pope.</note> were working openly with all their power to destroy the ideas that our blessed Reformers had given of their religion, and to erase the impressions that their expressive terms had made on the minds and hearts of the people. 

Those who have read the famous book by the Bishop of Condom <note>Referring to Jacques-Bénigne Bossuet (1627–1704), the "Eagle of Meaux," whose "Exposition of the Doctrine of the Catholic Church" (1671) sought to minimize the differences between Catholicism and Protestantism to encourage conversions.</note>, who has since been made Bishop of Meaux; those who have read the "Methods" that the Assembly of the Clergy prescribed to missionaries and controversialists; and especially those who for a very long time have seen these methods practiced before they were even published—those people, I say, know well that it was precisely at the time when Pastors were forbidden from speaking against the Roman Religion that it was most necessary to speak even more strongly against it. This was needed to render useless, by a kind of <term>counter-battery</term> <note>A military term for using one's own artillery to neutralize the enemy's cannons.</note>, the design that was undoubtedly already in place: to show our people the Roman Religion under a deceptive mask, hiding all its ugliness and all its deformities from their eyes, in order to take away, or at least gradually diminish, the aversion they felt for it.

When someone—even among those who had already fallen or who had entered into engagement with our enemies <note>Likely referring to those who had formally converted to Catholicism under duress.</note>—was sick, they were obliged to see him, and to make all the efforts of which they could be capable to put him back on the path of salvation.

In a word, they were obliged to fulfill all the other duties of their **Ministry**, without having any regard for the prohibitions that had been made to them, nor for the dangers to which they would have been exposed by refusing to obey them. However, it is certain that the fear of causing trouble for themselves had prevailed; and that most **Pastors** had not dared for a long time to contravene these kinds of prohibitions, once they had been served to them. From this comes, in my opinion, that being almost all accustomed for a long time to obey when it was necessary to disobey, they made no resistance to the **Edict** <term>Edict: The Edict of Fontainebleau (1685), which revoked the Edict of Nantes. It ordered all Protestant ministers to leave France within fifteen days or face the galleys.</term> which enjoined them to depart, although this obedience was manifestly contrary to all the duties of their **Ministry**.

One will perhaps say again that there would have been temerity in remaining in the **Kingdom** after an order like that; and that to act wisely one must avoid temptation as much as one can, because the flesh is as weak as the spirit is willing <note>A paraphrase of Matthew 26:41: "the spirit indeed is willing, but the flesh is weak."</note>. # Retreat of the Pastors of France.

<meta>catchword: If he</meta>

...exposed to all the dangers of a horrible persecution, or of failing in the most important duties of their Ministry, at a time when the interests of the glory of God and the public edification of His Church made these duties even more necessary. Consequently, they should have regarded this necessity to which they were reduced as a legitimate and sufficient calling <term>calling: original "vocation," in 17th-century theology, this refers to a divine summons or a clear path laid out by God’s will through circumstances.</term> to expose themselves to the dangers of which we speak; a calling which God Himself made clear to them, by the very fact that He had seen fit to reduce them to such a state. 

If the Author of the *Apology* <note>original: "l'Apologie"; likely referring to a contemporary text written to defend the ministers who fled France after the 1685 Revocation of the Edict of Nantes, which made Protestantism illegal.</note> had reflected well on this, he would not say, as he does, that *God’s Providence did not see fit to bring them to grips with* 
<margin>P. 105.</margin>
*death*; and he would not regard this final trial as the side by which *God did not* 
<margin>Ibid.</margin> <note>original: "Ibid."; an abbreviation of the Latin "ibidem," meaning "in the same place," referring to the same page or book previously cited.</note>
*see fit to test them.* For although the outcome has shown that God had not resolved to be glorified on this occasion by the death of the Pastors; that does not prevent His Providence from judging it appropriate that they expose themselves to the dangers of death to which He had led them Himself, by reducing them to the necessity of remaining exposed to this danger, or failing in the duties of their Ministry. God showed clearly enough by this that He saw fit to test them further by this final trial, as well as by the first ones. But to say things as they are, it is the Pastors themselves who did not see fit to withstand this trial, as they had withstood the others. They did not reflect on what God required of them on this occasion; or if they did reflect on it, they misunderstood what He wanted them to hear.

<meta>This page continues the critique of the Protestant pastors who fled France, arguing that their duty to stay was a divine mandate that should not have been ignored based on the "outcome" of events or personal safety.</meta>

<page-num>68</page-num>

# Disinterested Sentiments on the
<meta>catchword: vent</meta>

If it were permitted to judge things simply by their outcome—deciding what God finds fitting or unfitting for us to do based on what happens—we would only ever do what we wanted. We would avoid everything that caused us aversion, paying no mind to the rules of duty, and simply say afterward that God found it fitting (or not) for things to happen in that specific way. 

I say then once more, despite what actually occurred to the contrary, that God found it very fitting to test the Pastors through the ultimate dangers they would have faced by remaining in the Kingdom <note>The "Kingdom" refers to France under the reign of Louis XIV, specifically during the period following the 1685 Revocation of the Edict of Nantes, which made practicing the Protestant faith illegal and forced many ministers to choose between flight, conversion, or death.</note>; they were obligated to stay there. Those among them who, having taken this path, fell into the hands of persecutors, could never have been accused of rashly exposing themselves to these dangers, or of overestimating their own strength by seeking Martyrdom <term>Martyrdom: from the Greek "martys" (witness), the act of suffering or dying for one's religious beliefs rather than renouncing them.</term> without necessity. 

On the contrary, their <term>calling: original "vocation"; in 17th-century theology, this refers to a divine summons or a clear path of duty laid out by God’s will through one's station in life.</term> was so clear and evident that one cannot excuse the fault they committed by refusing to follow it, even when they were presented with the means to suddenly shield themselves from these dangers. To have fled rather than obeyed in this instance is not prudence, but a lack of trust in the grace of God. 

It is just as it is in the military: we only view as reckless or rash those who, out of sheer lightheartedness and without being commanded, go and expose themselves to the greatest dangers. But we never blame those who remain at the posts where they were stationed, regardless of the danger they find...

<summary>The author argues that Protestant ministers had a moral and divine obligation to stay with their flocks in France despite the danger of death. He asserts that fleeing from this duty demonstrates a lack of faith rather than "prudence," using a military analogy to emphasize that staying at one's post is an act of obedience, not recklessness.</summary>

One can very well compare the commitment of a **Pastor** <term>Pastor: In this context, a French Protestant (Huguenot) minister.</term> to his Church with that of a husband to his wife; since Jesus Christ, who is the great Pastor of the universal Church, so often regards it as his Bride: and it is well enough known that adultery dissolves a marriage, and breaks all its engagements, according to the maxim of the Gospel. However, if it happened that a woman fell into the hands of one or several brutes <note>The "brutes" or "ruffians" (brutaux) likely refers to the King’s dragoons, who used physical violence and intimidation to force Protestants to convert to Catholicism.</note> who, after having beaten her with many blows and inflicted several wounds upon her, had finally forced her through violence or threats to commit adultery with them; I do not believe that her husband—not having rescued her, or having been able to give her only useless help—would be permitted before equitable Judges to demand the dissolution of his marriage, even though she remained in the hands of these brutes, and that by the same motives of force or fear, they were still masters of her body without being masters of her heart.

I even believe that a reasonable and generous husband would be obliged to make every possible effort to deliver his wife from their hands and to be reunited with her, far from regarding his commitment as an engagement broken by an adultery of this nature. It is the same with the commitment of the Pastors we are speaking of, regarding their **Churches which have fallen.** <note>A "fallen Church" refers to a Protestant congregation that officially abjured its faith under the pressure of the Great Persecution.</note> It is certain that their fall can be regarded as a **spiritual adultery**; <note>Spiritual adultery: A common biblical metaphor where the turning away from God to another faith is compared to a spouse's infidelity.</note> and that if it had been voluntary and with the full consent of those who fell, it would have broken all the bonds

It will perhaps be said to me here that the Pastors cannot be regarded as the husbands of their Churches, but only as the servants of their husband, who is Jesus Christ; and that thus the comparison is not just. I answer to that, first, that although strictly speaking one cannot regard Pastors as the husbands of their Churches, that does not prevent one from legitimately comparing their engagement with them to that of a marriage, in order to draw from it the consequences that I have drawn. ...to whom he committed the care of these Churches, our comparison will have all the more force; because, in this regard, the **Pastors** <term>Pastor: A minister of the French Reformed (Protestant) Church.</term> are not the party offended by the fall of their **Churches** <term>Church: Here referring to the specific local congregation or flock.</term>, as they would be if they regarded them as their spouses; and this fall or this forced adultery, far from breaking the engagement they had to serve them, had obliged them to new efforts and to new cares for them; because of the account they must give to him who had committed to them the care of watching over their conduct; and who had established them in his house, to serve them with all their power.

One can very well compare the spiritual adultery <note>A theological term for idolatry or turning away from the true faith—here specifically referring to Protestants being forced to attend Catholic Mass.</note> of the Churches of France to the physical adultery of that famous Roman woman <note>Lucretia, a noblewoman in Roman legend whose rape by the son of King Tarquin led to the founding of the Roman Republic.</note>, who is proposed every day as an example of chastity and marital fidelity notwithstanding this adultery; because her heart had no part in it. Thus we can very well compare the **Pastors**, of whom we speak here, to the servants that her husband had left in his house, when the son of **Tarquin** <note>Sextus Tarquinius, the prince who assaulted Lucretia.</note> entered there to satisfy his brutal passion. Let us suppose that **Sextus**, upon entering or before entering that house, had ordered all of **Collatin's** <note>Lucretia's husband.</note> servants to leave and to leave **Lucretia** all alone, and that these servants, unable to be unaware of the design of this brute, had obeyed his orders, and abandoned their master's wife without help: or let us even suppose that they had only obeyed this order after having learned that the fear of death and the threats of Sextus had triumphed over the constancy of Lucretia.
