St. Athanasius <note>Athanasius of Alexandria (c. 296–373), a Church Father who famously fled several times during the Arian controversy to continue his work from hiding.</note> made an **Apology for his flight**, where he showed that Pastors can sometimes flee legitimately; and St. Augustine took a middle ground between these two opposing views. The Author of the Apology <note>Referring to Pierre Jurieu, whose "Apology for the Ministers" defended the Huguenot pastors' retreat from France.</note> reports here the reasons of both parties, to then draw his conclusion in favor of the retreat of the Pastors. Let us now see how he carries this out.

He first makes some reflections on the time at which Tertullian <note>A prolific early Christian author (c. 155–220) known for his rigorist views; his work "De Fuga in Persecutione" argued that Christians should not flee persecution.</note> wrote his treatise on flight during persecution, and on the motive that obliged him to write it, which was to strengthen those whom the persecution terrified. But finding nothing in the motive, nor in the time, nor in the occasion of this writing that <margin>P. 152.</margin> could serve his purpose, he passes to a more useful consideration on *Montanism* <term>Montanism: an early Christian movement characterized by a belief in new prophetic revelations and strict moral rigor, eventually deemed heretical by the mainstream church.</term> of which Tertullian makes an open profession in this work. He cites a passage from this writing where the *Paraclete* <note>original: "Paraclet." From the Greek for "advocate" or "helper," used in the Gospel of John for the Holy Spirit. In Montanism, it referred to the specific prophetic inspiration they claimed.</note> is mentioned, through which he shows that he was already a *Montanist* when he composed it; and he supports his view with the authority of St. Jerome, who says of him: "he is a man who does not belong to the Church," and who counts the book in question among those that Tertullian wrote against the Church.

After having thus discredited the name of this <margin>P. 153.</margin> Father, so that he might be counted for nothing here; he reports in brief the principal reasons by which he supports his opinion: and then refutes them one after another, at least those regarding the flight of all the faithful. # Retreat of the Pastors of France.

This Father <note>Referring to Saint Athanasius (c. 296–373 AD), a prominent Bishop of Alexandria who famously fled from his Arian persecutors.</note> supposes, just as *Tertullian* does, that the *passages which speak of fleeing from one place to another* <note>A reference to Matthew 10:23: "When they persecute you in one town, flee to the next."</note> constitute a *precept and a law*. Secondly, that *St. Athanasius*, as well as *Tertullian*, seeks the *number* as much as the *force of his reasons* <note>The author suggests Athanasius is prioritizing the quantity of his arguments over their logical strength.</note>. In the third place, he attaches himself more precisely to the *flight of those who are dedicated to the service of the Church*. He draws from his *first remark* this consequence: that, <margin>P. 182.</margin> assuming one is in the required circumstances, one must regard flight as an *act of obedience* for which there is no sufficient reason to exempt oneself when one has the means to obey. It should have been stated here again: "when one is in the case and in the required circumstances," and not "when one has the means to obey," because these terms are too general and do not follow from his principle. If he understands it thus, and with this restriction, I am of his opinion in this regard.

But I will show in what follows that *Tertullian* was not as wrong as one imagines when, regarding the passage in question as an *express order* and not as a *simple permission*, he limited the use of this *precept to the person of the Apostles and to the first years of their Ministry*. For although *St. Athanasius* is of an opposite opinion and did not respond to this difficulty that one might raise against him, it does not follow that one can legitimately draw <margin>P. 183.</margin> *the prejudice* <note>In this context, "prejudice" (French: *préjugé*) refers to a preliminary judgment or a presumption used as an argument.</note> *that our Author draws from this silence:* as if it proved that *the interpretation of the latter was universally received*. It could easily have happened that this Bishop had not seen this difficulty, or that, having seen it, he did not wish to propose it to himself for fear of being unable to resolve it, as happens quite

<summary>The author critiques Saint Athanasius's argument that fleeing persecution is a mandatory religious command, noting that Athanasius fails to address Tertullian's counter-argument that such commands were limited only to the original Apostles.</summary>

# Disinterested Sentiments on the...

...often to those who write or speak in favor of their own opinions, especially when they have some interest in having them accepted.

<margin>P. 184.</margin>
As his second remark only tends to excuse **St. Athanasius** for having applied several things to his subject which are irrelevant, and which are of no consequence to the matter he treats, I have nothing to say, except that this itself is a mark of his **bias** <note>The original French "prevention" refers to a preconceived opinion or prejudice that clouds judgment.</note>, which made him regard as proofs things that fundamentally prove nothing. I do not even know if this malady is not a bit contagious to those who treat this matter: for it seems to me that the Author of the *Apology for our Pastors* <note>This likely refers to a contemporary 17th-century work defending Protestant ministers (pastors) who fled France during the persecutions surrounding the Revocation of the Edict of Nantes.</note> appears somewhat affected by it in several places in his work; he who, in everything else, shows himself to be most enlightened and most capable of solid and convincing reasoning. As for **St. Athanasius**, it is certain that this clearly shows that one must carefully examine his reasons on this subject before admitting them; for fear of receiving as proofs things that would only have the appearance of them, and which would be good at most only for *those minds which never yield to solid and convincing reasons, and which allow themselves to be easily surprised by the false lights of a sophism or a childish argument.*

Regarding his third general remark, there is something to be said about how, after having stated that what precisely concerns the **flight of the Pastors** was properly the affair of **St. Athanasius**—who had found a way to escape when **Syrianus** <note>Syrianus was a Roman military commander in Egypt who led a night-time raid on the church of St. Theonas in 356 AD to arrest Athanasius.</note> was sent to seize him—he adds that he had left by his retreat his

# Retreat of the Pastors of France.

*Church exposed to great vexations.* These are somewhat ambiguous terms that might lead those who are not well-acquainted with the circumstances of this event to think that the retreat of this **Holy Bishop** <note>The author refers to Saint Athanasius (c. 296–373 AD), the Bishop of Alexandria and a chief defender of Trinitarianism.</note> was the cause of the vexations subsequently inflicted upon his Church. However, it is certain that if anything were capable of delivering the faithful of **Alexandria** from the vexations that followed this retreat, it should have been—by all appearances—the absence of this illustrious Pastor. He was the principal object of the fury of the **Arians** <note>Followers of Arius, who taught that Jesus Christ was a created being and not co-eternal with God the Father. This was the era's most significant theological conflict.</note>, and the only one against whom the **Emperor Constantius** <note>Constantius II, who ruled the Eastern Roman Empire and was sympathetic to Arianism.</note> had dispatched nearly five thousand men commanded by Syrianus, to take him dead or alive. The reason for this was that this Emperor harbored a particular resentment against **St. Athanasius** because of the complaints he had made to **Constans** his brother, Emperor of the West, against the Arians whom Constantius openly favored. For, upon these complaints, Constans had written him a letter which obliged him to assemble the **Council of Sardica** <note>Held in 343 AD in modern-day Sofia, Bulgaria, to resolve the Arian controversy and the deposition of Athanasius.</note> where the Arians were condemned, and **St. Athanasius** along with the Bishops of his party were justified. <margin>Theodoret, Ecclesiastical History, Book 2, Chapter 8.</margin> After the holding of this Council, he had written him a second letter full of vigor, which contained not only a prayer and an exhortation but even threats; the principal one being that if he would not grant his request and render the justice he demanded, he would go to Alexandria himself, restore Athanasius to the people who passionately desired him, and drive out his enemies. Constantius had been obliged at the time to grant his brother what he asked in this letter, in order to avoid the civil war that a refusal

I do not know how the Author of the *Apology for the Pastors* could have considered *the history of this particular event* that St. Athanasius <note>Athanasius (c. 296–373 AD) was the Bishop of Alexandria and a chief defender of Trinitarianism; he was forced into exile five times.</note> relates at some length, and how he could have noticed that he counts among the excuses for his retreat the pressing prayers of the People, and the violence done to him by the Monks and Priests of Alexandria to pull him away from there as if by force, without paying attention to the consequences that one can draw from it against the Pastors <note>Refers to the French Huguenot ministers who fled France following the Revocation of the Edict of Nantes in 1685.</note> who, in a lesser danger and in a lesser trouble, beyond comparison, did not wait nearly long enough for someone to do them a similar violence. # Retreat of the Pastors of France.

„ the cohorts having entered the Church <note>original: "Temple"; in this context, Athanasius refers to the cathedral or basilica in Alexandria, though French Protestant writers often used "Temple" for their own churches.</note> by force; and the soldiers having surrounded the sacred place to seize me, all those who remained, both of the Clergy and of the People, began to cry out and beg me earnestly to withdraw. Therefore, having left my chair, I redoubled my prayers to them to urge them to hurry out; Because it was better that I should present myself to the danger than if any of them should suffer harm. Many then departed, and as the others followed them, my Monks and some of the Clergy dragged me away with them as they were leaving. It was thus, and I bear witness to the truth, that, the Lord being our guide and our protector, we passed, without any harm, through the midst of the soldiers, some of whom were surrounding the sacred place, and others were going here and there in the Church. We escaped them without their noticing it: <margin>It would be desirable if the Pastors of the Churches of France could say the same thing today.</margin> *Praising and glorifying God because we had not* * *abandoned the people,* <note>original: "abandonné le peuple"</note> *and because, having first sent them away without any harm, we had subsequently avoided falling into the hands of those who were seeking us.* Since, then, the Providence of God withdrew me from this danger in such a marvelous manner, I do not believe there is anyone who can, with good reason, make it a crime that I did not then retrace my steps to present myself before the eyes,

„ and to deliver myself into the hands of those who were seeking me.

If the Pastors of the Churches of France had been as scrupulous as St. Athanasius; and if they had possessed for their Flocks the same affection that he showed then for his own, it is certain that they would not have hurried so quickly to leave the Kingdom and abandon them to the mercy of the **monstrous wolves** <note>original: "loups garous"; literally "werewolves," a pejorative term used here to describe the fierce persecutors or the "dragoons" tasked with forcing conversions of Protestants.</note> whom they saw ready to devour them. One can say of the situation in which this Holy Bishop then found himself, <margin>P. 92.</margin> that it was literally one of those where it is a case of *every man for himself* <note>original: "sauve qui peut"</note>: however, we do not see that he thought only of *extricating himself from trouble*, without *worrying about the others*: on the contrary, he worried about others much more than himself. He could not resolve to abandon the people in such great tribulation, because he regarded such a step as shameful; and he believed it much *more just that he remain exposed to danger himself, than to think of his own safety before having provided for that of the others.* Thereupon, the Leader and the soldiers who had orders to seize him entered with impetuosity; they pressed him, they begged him earnestly to leave and escape from their hands; and he, on the contrary, persisted in his resolution, and protested that he would not leave until they had all gone out, down to the very last one. Finally, his friends dragged him out of the Temple <note>original: "Temple"; a term often used by French Protestants for their churches, here applied to Athanasius’s church in Alexandria.</note> and delivered him from the danger, as if against his will; and after this fortunate deliverance, he found no consolation in his retreat except for the fact that he had not abandoned the people, and that before fleeing himself, he *had sent them away without any harm.*

# Retreat of the Pastors of France.

In all honesty, can anyone believe, after all this, that Saint Athanasius <note>A 4th-century Bishop of Alexandria known for defending orthodox Christianity against Arianism.</note> would find it acceptable today for his retreat to be compared with that of our Pastors? He was being persecuted personally, and they were specifically seeking only his life to satisfy the unjust resentment of an Emperor and the malice of the **Arians** <note>Followers of Arius, who held beliefs about the nature of Christ that conflicted with the established Church; they were political and religious rivals to Athanasius.</note> who incited this Prince to vengeance; these others <note>Referring to the French Protestant ministers (Huguenots).</note> were only exposed to a general persecution that was common to all their Flocks. The former found himself trapped in a specific **Church** <note>original: "Temple"; in French Protestant tradition, their places of worship were called "temples" to distinguish them from Catholic "églises."</note> that five thousand **armed guards** <note>original: "satellites"; refers to the soldiers or attendants of the Emperor.</note> had surrounded to capture him; the latter were still in their houses or in the countryside, and only saw personal danger from afar, at least for the most part. The former could not then stay at his Church without being an occasion for even greater trouble and greater persecution; while these others, without increasing the severity of the persecution, could have, by staying near their Churches, strengthened many of them and shared with them, as much as possible, the consolations they so greatly needed in the deplorable state to which they were reduced. However, the former, far from hurrying to leave the Church where he saw himself personally exposed to such a great and pressing danger, could not resolve to take that path until after having seen the departure of most of those who were caught there with him, and the rest in a position to get out similarly; even then, a kind of **force** <note>original: "violence"</note> on the part of his own people was necessary to resolve him to it; and these others have taken the resolution to leave the Kingdom at the time when their Flocks were still there, or

## 102.
### ->Disinterested Sentiments on the<-

...ing <note>Completing the word "reasoning" from the previous page.</note> that I have just reported, I believe that nothing more would be needed to convince everyone of it; and I am persuaded that if he wishes to take the trouble to examine it himself, without prejudice, it will not be difficult for him to perceive its defects, and to see that he regards as proofs things that prove nothing at heart <note>original: "au fons"; fundamentally or essentially.</note>.

In effect, it is not a question here of knowing whether it is permitted for the **Ministers of the Gospel** <note>Protestant clergymen.</note> to escape the hands of their persecutors; but it is a question of knowing whether it is permitted for them to abandon the **Churches** or the **Flocks** <note>The congregations of the faithful.</note> that God has committed to their care, and to place themselves under cover from persecution, while these Flocks and these Churches remain exposed to it. These are two very different things: for if it were simply a matter of the first, without any connection to the second, one could not reasonably contest the right of the **Pastors** to use this permission, and their flight would have been very legitimate. However, the Author of the **Apology** <note>original: "Apologie"; a formal written defense of the pastors' actions.</note> separates these two things in the reasoning that I have just reported, and to better suit his purpose, he speaks only of escaping the hands of persecutors when they are cruel, cunning, artful, unjust, violent, etc.; whereas it was necessary to join to this retreat the abandonment of their Churches, which is precisely what makes it illegitimate.

It is true that this is very subtly and very skillfully done by him; for, if he had joined these two things together, he would not have been able to so easily draw his conclusions, which, although they have no force in relation to the

## Retreat of the Pastors of France.

...fundamentally <note>original: "au fons"; completing the thought from the previous page's catchword "au-".</note> the question properly at issue, nonetheless create some illusion because of the word "retreat" <note>Here meaning "flight" or "withdrawal" from one's post.</note>. This is what makes me believe that he found it good to follow in this the method of the *Ancient Doctors* <note>The Church Fathers of the early Christian centuries.</note>, of whom I have already spoken, and that he ventured this reasoning for those superficial minds *who never yield to solid and convincing reasons, and who let themselves be easily surprised by the false lights of a Sophism.* <note>A clever but false argument.</note>

I will say then, in order to destroy the impressions that this might have made, that St. Athanasius <note>A 4th-century Bishop of Alexandria who famously fled several times to escape persecution by the Arians.</note> said nothing in his Apology, and apparently never thought anything, *that gives reason to conclude that when the Ministers of the Gospel have to deal with hypocritical, treacherous enemies of a character similar to that of the Arians who persecuted him, they may innocently abandon their Flocks, leaving them without a Pastor in the time of their affliction: moving away from them, and putting themselves out of danger, as soon as they see the beginnings of the cruelties that afflict them.* On the contrary, he said several things from which one can draw directly opposite consequences: and he acted in a manner that shows his thoughts were altogether removed from those which the Author of the Apology wants to skillfully pass off under his name. To be fully convinced of this, one has only to cast an eye upon the account he gives of his flight, which I have already reported, and principally upon these words; *For my part, I believed then that it would be an unseemly thing for me to abandon the people in such great tribulation: and that it was more just to brave the danger for the...*

# Retreat of the Pastors of France.

done here, there are the Pastors of France suddenly justified by the character of those who drove them out. For one can certainly see in them all the hypocrisy, treachery, malice, cruelty, violence, and every other kind of injustice that has ever been blamed on the ancient persecutors of the Church.

<margin>Page 186.</margin>
As for the rest, since I do not claim that the Pastors should have remained in France without taking any precautions to prevent themselves from falling into the hands of their enemies; it is not necessary for me to respond here to what the Author of the *Apology* <note>The author refers to a contemporary defense of the refugee pastors, likely the "Apologie pour les pasteurs de France" (1688).</note> says—that the Gospel does not forbid anywhere the taking of precautions against betrayals and frauds, that it even recommends prudence in several places, and that it is primarily to the Pastors that Jesus Christ speaks when he says to his Disciples, *I send you, etc. Be therefore prudent, etc.* <note>Matthew 10:16: "Behold, I send you out as sheep in the midst of wolves. Therefore be wise as serpents and harmless as doves."</note> One can see well that this does not go against my sentiments; for I am very much persuaded that it is permitted to all men, and that they are even obliged, in order to follow the rules of wisdom and common sense, to take all the precautions they can legitimately take against betrayals and frauds, and to follow in all things the advice given by prudence, provided that this prudence be a **Christian and Evangelical prudence**, and not a **carnal prudence**. One must take great care to distinguish these two kinds of prudence, because the carnal kind <note>The author defines "carnal prudence" as worldly self-interest or a survival instinct that overrides religious duty.</note> could often give us pernicious advice contrary to our duty, provided that it found there some advantage, or some

temporal safety <note>In this 17th-century context, "temporal safety" refers to the preservation of one's physical life and worldly belongings, often contrasted with "eternal safety" or spiritual duty.</note>: whereas **Evangelical prudence** <term>Evangelical prudence: Wisdom based on the teachings of the Gospel, which suggests that one should be cautious but never at the expense of one's religious obligations.</term> never gives us such advice, and it teaches us, on the contrary, to follow the rules of our duty in all things, and to expose ourselves to every kind of evil rather than to fail in that duty in any way. ### Retreat of the Pastors of France.

[the author of the Apology] <note>Referring to the *Apologie des Pasteurs de France*, a defense of the Huguenot ministers who fled after the Revocation of the Edict of Nantes in 1685.</note> could very well have done without [this argument] without harming himself, and without harming those for whom he makes this Apology. He says that since we do not know if God calls us to die when He presents us at the same time with a danger that threatens us and a door through which to escape, it is, it seems, evident that we must make use of it as in all similar occasions. These similar occasions are, according to him: when one defends oneself against an assassin who attacks our life, when one puts out a fire in a burning house, the cultivation of land, the building of houses, or the undertaking of journeys. For after having made a great argument to prove that one can legitimately do all these things, even though one is ignorant of what the outcome of all these designs will be, he draws this consequence: *“Thus, when Providence gives us a choice between danger and flight, the uncertainty we are in regarding His secret intentions obliges us to prefer flight over danger, etc.”*

I say to all this that the Author of the Apology is much mistaken when he believed that occasions to flee danger are the same as those of defending oneself against an assassin, of putting out the fire of a burning house, or others of that nature; because it is always permissible and necessary to defend oneself against an assassin, to put out a fire, etc., and because there are several occasions in life where it is not permitted to flee danger, and where it is even legitimately commanded to expose oneself to it or to remain exposed to it. It would be a fine thing to see <note>The author uses this ironically to mean "It would be absurd to see."</note> soldiers refuse to expose themselves to dangers in the events of war; or that, having abandoned their posts, they would wish to allege for their excuse that the uncertainty they were in regarding the

### Disinterested Feelings on the...

secret intentions of Providence has forced them to prefer flight over danger. Everyone sees clearly enough how frivolous these excuses would be. It is the same with our Pastors: the uncertainty they were in regarding the secret intentions of Providence in no way obliged them to prefer flight over danger: on the contrary, they were obliged in this uncertainty to prefer danger over flight, because they had a certain knowledge of the laws of their duty, which obliged them to remain exposed to danger for the edification, consolation, or restoration of their Churches; and nothing more was needed to convince them and to force them to believe that God wanted them to remain in danger.

When I speak here of remaining in danger, I mean a general danger, such as that which all the Pastors would have faced who remained in France, despite the edict <note>The Edict of Fontainebleau, issued by Louis XIV in 1685, which revoked the Edict of Nantes. It ordered all Protestant pastors to leave the kingdom within fifteen days or face the galleys.</note> which enjoined them to leave it, and not at all the particular dangers to which each might have been exposed from day to day, and against which I have already remarked that they could have taken all possible precautions, by following the advice of a Christian and Evangelical prudence. And when I speak of the flight to which one must prefer danger, I mean an absolute flight accompanied by a total desertion or an entire abandonment of the Flocks, as was that of our Pastors. For if they had done nothing but flee from one place to another when they were sought or pursued personally, without abandoning their Churches and without straying any more than was...

### Retreat of the Pastors of France

One must therefore clearly distinguish these things, and not do as our Author, who confuses them everywhere, and who makes, because of that, so many reflections and so many reasonings entirely useless to his subject.

Moreover, although he founds the reflections of which we have just spoken on those that St. Athanasius <note>St. Athanasius of Alexandria (c. 296–373 AD) was a prominent Greek Church Father known for his defense against Arianism; he famously fled his see several times to avoid capture.</note> made touching *the uncertainty of the time that God has determined for our death*; it is certain that he has moved far from the intention of this Father, who apparently had nothing less in view than the consequences that our Author has drawn from it. This Holy Bishop, after having represented how Jesus Christ, knowing that his hour had come, no longer avoided falling into the hands of those who sought him to put him to death, as he had avoided it before on some occasions, because the time of his sufferings had not yet come; and after having remarked that far from avoiding them, he had offered himself to them, and in some manner delivered himself into their hands. After that, I say, he opposes to this conduct of the Son of God that of the Sainted men who, before or after him, avoided falling into the hands of their persecutors; whether by flight, or by hiding and stealing away from their searches. He gives this reason for it: that *these latter, being simply only men, and not knowing what was the time that Providence had prescribed for them, did not go to place themselves in the power of those who [sought]* <note>The text ends mid-sentence; the catchword "dressoient" suggests the next page begins with "were laying [snares] for them."</note>

-># Sentiments of the Disinterested on the<-
<note>The header refers to the title of the work, "Disinterested Sentiments on the Retreat of the Pastors of France," likely part of a polemic concerning the Huguenot ministers who fled after the Revocation of the Edict of Nantes in 1685.</note>

[those who] laid ambushes for them; because they knew well that the fate of men is in the hands of **God**. The reflection he makes on the uncertainty, or rather on the complete ignorance in which we find ourselves regarding the time that God has determined for our death, is therefore only to show that we must not imitate the example of **Jesus Christ**, in how he delivered himself up to those who sought him to put him to death; because there is this difference between him and us: he knew the time God had determined for the duration of his days on earth, and he was the absolute Master of his life to give it up and take it back according to his good pleasure; whereas we do not know the former, and we do not have, as he did, the right to dispose of our days. For the days of man are in the hand of God, who has the power to cause death just as he has the power to give life, according to the thought of this Holy Bishop <note>original: "S. Evêque," referring to St. Athanasius.</note>. He was, moreover, so far from believing that it is always permitted for Pastors to escape danger through the door that is open to them, when they see a danger threatening them on one side and an open door to escape on the other, that he himself says that when the time calls them to it, or when they are captured, they must fight until the end for the Church. Here is the entire passage, by which one may more clearly understand the thought of this Father. *This Law*, he says, *was given to all: that during persecution they should flee when they are pursued, that they should hide when they are sought, and that they should not be eager to tempt the Lord rashly; but that they should wait until the time that has been* <note>The text cuts off here, continuing the quote on the next page.</note>

<summary>This page argues that unlike Jesus Christ, who had divine authority over his own life and death, ordinary humans and pastors must respect God's providence and not seek martyrdom rashly. It cites St. Athanasius to argue that while flight is sometimes a "Law" during persecution, it does not absolve pastors of their ultimate duty to the Church when captured.</summary>

# Retreat of the Pastors of France. #

[...that the time] determined for death has come, or that this Judge <note>Referring to God as the ultimate judge of life and death.</note> has ordered of them what seems good to him. However, he willed that we should always keep ourselves ready, so that, whether the time calls us to it, or whether we are captured, we fight until the end for the Church.

I find these terms very remarkable, *whether the time calls us to it*, for they clearly show that according to ***St. Athanasius***, there are times and circumstances that engage Pastors to fight until the end for the Church. These times and circumstances can undoubtedly only be the times of persecution in which the Ministry of Pastors is more necessary to the Church; and I am persuaded that if ***St. Athanasius*** had been able to foresee the persecution that afflicted our Churches, and the deplorable state in which they found themselves reduced when the Pastors left the Kingdom <note>This refers to the mass exodus of Huguenot ministers following the Revocation of the Edict of Nantes in 1685, which made Protestantism illegal in France.</note>, he would have undoubtedly regarded this time as one of those which should most obviously and most strongly call the Pastors to fight until the end for their Churches: because it would be difficult, in my opinion, to imagine a time and circumstances more capable of indispensably engaging them in this duty.

<margin>P. 191.</margin>

### Disinterested Sentiments on the ###

it clearly shows that this Holy Bishop <note>Referring to St. Athanasius (c. 296–373 AD), a prominent Church Father known for his defense against Arianism.</note> understands by the flight, of which he speaks in his *Apology*, only that which does not go as far as total desertion; such as the flight of those who only flee a present danger to expose themselves the next day to another, by continuing without rest the work of their Ministry, for the advancement of the glory of God, and for the edification of his Church.

<margin>P. 192.</margin>
It is for this reason that he regards it *as a laborious exercise, and as a kind of middle path that the faithful follow, both in order not to present themselves voluntarily—which would be to kill oneself and make oneself guilty of one's own death, etc.—and so as not to incur the blame of a criminal timidity, as if they were afraid of the afflictions of flight, which are more heavy and more painful than death itself.* It is for this reason that he also says *that he who is dead has no more pain to endure, but that he who flees, expecting every day new attacks from his enemies, would find death much more bearable.* I am surprised that the Author of the *Apology of the Pastors* <note>A reference to contemporary writings defending the Huguenot ministers who fled France after the Revocation of the Edict of Nantes in 1685.</note> did not perceive here that these terms—*expecting every day new attacks from his enemies*—cannot apply to the flight, or rather to the retreat, of those who, upon leaving the Kingdom <note>France.</note>, were furnished with good passports and went to place themselves in a state of safety, far from the battles and the reach of the enemies who drove them out. These enemies asked for nothing more than their removal so they could devour, with less resistance, all the Flocks whose care and conduct God had committed to them. ## Retreat of the Pastors of France. 121

...is that which concerns the miracle God performed in favor of **Saint Peter**, when He sent an Angel to draw him out of prison and to set him free to withdraw elsewhere. The author of the *Apology for the Pastors* draws this <margin>Page 198.</margin> conclusion from it: that it clearly appears from this that God is not an enemy of the flight of His ministers, since He favors it with miracles. He adds that Saint Peter did not believe it was forbidden for him to flee; that he made use of the freedom God gave him; that he withdrew to a place where he could be in safety; that he did not return to put himself back in prison, nor to resume his chains; that **Saint Paul** also did not return to Damascus after he had escaped by a ruse <note>In the Book of Acts, Paul escaped Damascus by being lowered over the city wall in a basket to avoid a plot by his enemies.</note>; nor even **Dionysius of Alexandria** <note>A 3rd-century Bishop of Alexandria who survived several persecutions; he was once forcibly rescued by his followers and remained in hiding to continue his work.</note>, he who had made such a fuss so as not to consent to the violence of his liberators; he did not believe himself obliged to return and place himself in the power of his enemies once he had been led, as if by force, to a place of safety.

Finally, he responds in advance to those who <margin>Page 199.</margin> might say to this: that when an Angel comes to take a man by the hand and lead him out of danger, flight is not forbidden to him; but that it is not the same when God does not declare His will through a miracle. He responds, I say, that the miracle is irrelevant to the question; that it does not change the nature of the matter; that the will of God can be revealed without a miracle; and that when, through the secret workings of His **Providence** <note>The protective care of God; here, the author argues that natural opportunities for escape are just as much a sign of God's will as supernatural ones.</note>, He presents someone with clear and certain ways to avoid an extreme misfortune, He tells that man in a strong enough manner that he is permitted to take them.

# Opinions of the concerned parties on the

[A]pology for a flight such as that of **St. Athanasius** <note>Athanasius of Alexandria (c. 296–373 AD) was a Church Father who famously fled into the desert to escape the Arian persecutions. He wrote his own "Apology for his Flight" to defend himself against charges of cowardice.</note>, and who would write against those who would want to pass off every kind of flight as illegitimate. But, with regard to the retreat of our Pastors, the Author of the *Apology* will allow me to tell him that I regard almost all these latest arguments, as well as those of this nature that I have already examined, as shots fired into the air with a light heart, which strike and can wound no one. For who is the man of good sense in the world, who could say that one must remain in the hands and at the discretion of his enemies, when one finds a legitimate means of delivering oneself from them; or that when one has been happily delivered, one must go and put oneself back in their power? Certainly, I do not believe there are any such men, nor that there could be; and I am persuaded, on the contrary, that everyone agrees that as much as it is possible, one must avoid all the evils that can be avoided by legitimate precautions. I have already explained myself on this several times in my own regard, and I do not see that all the arguments of which I speak here do anything either against me, or against any reasonable person. It is true that the Author of the *Apology* adroitly insinuates two things there which could be of consequence, if they remained in the terms in which he has put them; but I hope that he himself will not refuse to correct them, when I have made him perceive what there is to remove or to add, and that thus we shall remain in agreement on everything in this regard. The second thing he must correct, if I am not mistaken, is what he says: that when <margin>P. 199.</margin> *Providence presents someone with clear and certain ways to avoid an extreme misfortune, God tells him thereby, in a sufficiently strong manner, that he is permitted to take them.* This proposition is fallacious <note>The author uses "captieuse" (captious), meaning an argument intended to deceive or entrap the listener through a sophisticated but false logic.</note> in that it supposes that all clear and certain ways to avoid an extreme misfortune are ways presented by the Providence of God. This is undoubtedly false when these ways, however clear and certain they may be, are not legitimate. For although it is true to say that Providence sometimes permits men to deliver themselves from the evils they endure, or those they fear, by illegitimate ways, it is nonetheless not true to say that it is God’s Providence that presented these ways to them. On the contrary, it is certain that Providence has naturally closed them off; because an illegitimate way of avoiding a misfortune is contrary to the laws of God's Providence, for the very reason that it is illegitimate. One can certainly say, for example, that God’s Providence formerly permitted Saul <note>King Saul, the first king of Israel, who turned away from God's commands.</note> to go consult the Witch <note>original: "Pythonisse." This refers to the Medium or Witch of Endor whom Saul consulted in 1 Samuel 28, a practice strictly forbidden by religious law.</note>, but one cannot say that it was Providence that had presented him with this way to inform himself of the outcome of the war he then had with the Philistines; on the contrary, it is certain that Providence had closed it to him, for the very reason that it was contrary to His laws. One must therefore take care

...care, when one wants to bring the <term>Providence of God</term> <note>The concept that God intervenes in the world and guides human affairs.</note> into the conduct or actions of men, not to attribute to it things that do not suit it in any way, and which could give occasion to wrongly cast upon it the faults for which men alone are guilty. There are clear, certain, and infallible ways to avoid the misfortunes that men regard as the greatest, and as the final extremities, which Providence never presents; because they are illegitimate; and such is—to give an example that has some relation to our subject—such is the means that ancient persecutors offered in the past, and that modern persecutors offer every day to <term>Confessors</term> <note>Those who maintain their faith under persecution without being killed.</note> and <term>Martyrs</term>, to abandon the profession of the <term>Gospel</term>, to avoid the torments they make them endure, or the death they prepare for them. There are, on the contrary, uncertain and doubtful, but legitimate ways, which one can follow and regard as ways presented by the Providence of God. Thus, it is not by the characteristics of certainty, clarity, or infallibility, but by the characteristic of legitimacy that one can know which ways are presented by the Providence of God. These are the very words I included in that letter. These terms, *for fear that we might abandon the Churches we are duty-bound to serve*, were, it seems to me, of enough consequence to the subject to deserve being considered, and I do not know why our Author did not find it appropriate to offer some reflection upon them.

I have included several other passages from the same letter, through which one can easily see what this Holy Doctor’s <note>Saint Augustine (354–430 AD), a foundational theologian whose writings on the duties of clergy during the Vandal invasion were central to this 17th-century debate.</note> sentiments were on this subject, and upon which I will also make some reflections before refuting what the Author of the *Apology* <note>Pierre Jurieu, who argued that French Protestant pastors were right to flee to safety after the Revocation of the Edict of Nantes in 1685.</note> says throughout his detailed examination of his reasons.

Bishop **Honoratus**, who believed he could legitimately flee and abandon his Flock without crime, made use of the passage from **St. Matthew 10** <note>Matthew 10:23: "When they persecute you in one town, flee to the next."</note> which commands one to flee from one city to another; the example of **Jesus Christ** who was carried into Egypt; and that of **St. Paul** when he fled from Damascus. **St. Augustine**, to respond to these objections, says firstly that neither Jesus Christ, by the order he gave to his Disciples, nor by the example of his own flight, intended for the Churches to remain deprived of the **Holy Ministry**. For, he says, *who is there who would believe that the Lord wished to permit flight in such a way that the Flocks he acquired by his blood remain deprived of the Ministry which is necessary to them, and without which they could not live.* Regarding the example of **St. Paul**, after having noted that this Holy Apostle was personally persecuted, he adds *let the Ministers of the word and the Sacraments therefore do in all things what he has commanded or permitted; let them flee from one city to another when any of them is specifically sought out by the persecutors, provided that the Church is not abandoned by those who are not thus pursued.* . . . .

# Withdrawal of the Pastors of France.

...there have been some who have abandoned their people; it is this that we have said must not be done. <header>The Retreat of the Pastors of France.</header>

...following his maxims, a **Pastor** <note>A term for a Protestant minister.</note> must never leave his **Church** deprived of this **Ministry** <note>The spiritual duties and services provided by a religious leader.</note>, whatever danger there may be in continuing it for them. Our **Pastors** certainly were not in this case; on the contrary, everyone knows that by abandoning their **Flocks** <note>A common metaphor for a congregation or church community.</note>, they left them without **Ministry**. Thus it is evident that, according to **St. Augustine**, it was not permitted for them to abandon them, even if the persecution had targeted them specifically, and the persecutors had only wanted their persons. But, far from that, they were precisely in the case of which this ancient **Doctor** <note>Referring to Saint Augustine (354–430 AD), a "Doctor of the Church" whose theological writings were considered authoritative for centuries.</note> speaks immediately after; since the danger threatening the **Church**, and the persecution she endured, concerned the **Flocks** at least as much as the **Pastors**. It is on this point that **St. Augustine** declares himself in the strongest and most express manner possible, by establishing this general maxim that I have already reported: *“when the danger is common to all, those who have need of help must not be abandoned by those whose help is necessary to them.”* <note>Original French: "lors que le danger est commun à tous, il ne faut pas que ceux qui ont besoin de secours, soient abandonnés par ceux dont le secours leur est necessaire."</note> I see nothing more positive nor more formal against the conduct of the **Pastors** who have left **France**, when the same danger threatened and the same persecution attacked all the **Churches** that **God** had gathered there. These were incontestably evils *common to all*, not only to the Clergy in general, following the case mentioned by **St. Augustine**; but also to all their **Flocks**, who needed their spiritual help more than ever. For in the works of our **vocation** <note>In this context, "vocation" refers to a divine calling or professional appointment to the ministry.</note>, we must never look at what God *can* do without us as a pretext for laxity or abandonment; and we must always look at what we *must* do for him, or for our neighbors, as a law that imposes upon us the necessity of working without cease, and which forbids us from ever abandoning this work to which we have been called, whatever difficulty may be encountered there.

But even if **St. Augustine** had only spoken—following the thought of our Author—of times when the danger is uncertain, one might well say, it seems to me, that there are at least as many reasons that can hinder persecutors as there are those that can hinder an enemy from reaching those excesses of inhumanity that are usually feared from either. However, to say things as I think them, I believe that in these kinds of occasions, to do well, one must consult only the laws of duty, and not at all the certainty or uncertainty of the dangers to which one may be exposed, or the extremities to which one might find oneself reduced. His maxim is that *when there is as much reason to hope that the evil will not happen, as to fear that it will happen, those whose presence is necessary for the consolation of others must think carefully about the consequences of their retreat; and examine well whether it is not advised by an unfounded fear.* 

But would he really want it to be the case that when there is more reason to fear than to hope, those whose presence is necessary for the consolation of others should not think carefully about the consequences of their retreat? <note>The "retreat" refers to the act of a pastor leaving his flock during a time of persecution.</note> And could he believe that it is only an unfounded fear, or a panic terror, that could make this retreat illegitimate? In all honesty, I cannot believe that this is his thought, although one could draw this consequence from his maxim; and I am persuaded that when he wishes to take the trouble to reflect on it without prejudice, he will find that the law of duty is always the best counselor one can take; and that on the contrary, one must always regard as very suspect, on these occasions, all

<meta>This page continues the critique of the "Apology" mentioned on page 155. The author is dissecting the hypothetical scenario proposed by the exiled Pastor: that if there were only one place on earth to serve God, staying there would be "perhaps" reasonable. The author argues this "perhaps" is a weak and insulting qualification for what should be a clear moral duty.</meta>

<page-num>156</page-num>

<header>Disinterested Sentiments on the</header> <note>The full title is likely *Disinterested Sentiments on the Retreat of the Pastors of France*.</note>

[would] prefer to expose themselves to every extremity <term>extremity: in this context, the most severe hardships, including physical suffering, loss of property, or death.</term> rather than leave that sanctified place, would it have something reasonable about it, and something to which the Pastor would be obliged to conform? I do not think he has reflected well on this *perhaps*, which he inserted into his proposition, nor on this *something reasonable*, etc. These are in effect terms that give rise to an alternative, of which I do not doubt he himself has horror, and which I have no intention of attributing to him. They are usually used only in problematic questions, and never in propositions believed to be of incontestable evidence, and above all contradiction. I do not believe there is any Christian who would want to say that, supposing there were only a single place on earth where it was possible to serve God, the zeal <note>original: *zele*</note> of those who would rather expose themselves to every extremity than leave that sanctified place, would *perhaps* have nothing reasonable about it, or that it would *perhaps* have something that would not be reasonable. 

However, that is the alternative in the proposition made by our Author; but I am persuaded that he will have no difficulty in correcting it, and in saying without hesitation regarding his supposition, that in that case the zeal of those of whom he speaks would be entirely reasonable: that the Pastors would be obliged to conform to it; that on the contrary, the lukewarmness <term>lukewarmness: a state of spiritual indifference or lack of fervor; here, the sin of being neither "hot nor cold" in one's faith.</term> of those who would rather leave that sanctified place and pass into places where it would be impossible to serve God, than expose themselves to every extremity, would be a damnable lukewarmness; and that the cowardice <note>original: *lâcheté*</note> of the Pastor or Pastors who would also take that path would be a cowar—

He contrasts the "zeal" of the faithful who stay with the "cowardice" and "lukewarmness" of ministers who flee.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author’s rebuttal of a text titled the "Apology," which defended the flight of French Protestant ministers (Huguenots) following the Revocation of the Edict of Nantes. The previous page ended by arguing that even the slightest doubt regarding the duty to serve God in any circumstance is an "abominable" thought.</meta>

<page-num>157</page-num>
# Retreat of the Pastors of France.
G 7
<meta>catchword: not</meta>

...[such an] abominable thing. There is undoubtedly no kind of danger, misfortune, nor extremity <term>extremity: a situation of extreme necessity or danger, often involving the risk of death or loss of all possessions.</term>, however certain it may be, that men—and especially the faithful—should ever put in parallel with the impossibility or non-possibility of serving God, which carries with it infinite misfortunes, to which all temporal misfortunes and all extremities to which one can be reduced on earth, have nothing comparable.

The Author of the *Apology* adds that, within the liberty that the Gospel gives us to serve God everywhere equally, he cannot understand that a Pastor <note>original: *Pasteur*; in the context of the French Reformed Church, this refers to a Protestant minister.</note> should be obliged to depend on the caprice of his Church, nor to sacrifice himself for the temporal interests of his Flock, when he prefers to remain in peril in one place rather than go to another where he will preserve for himself the advantage of serving God peacefully. To this I say, that there would be nothing difficult to understand if our Author had not himself introduced the difficulties by assuming what is not the case. For those who want the Pastor to remain, when the Flock <note>The "Flock" refers to the local congregation of Protestant believers.</note> takes the resolution to remain, do not claim that they depend on the caprice of their Churches, nor that they sacrifice themselves for the temporal interests of their Flocks; but only that they follow the rules of their duty and that they sacrifice themselves for the salvation of souls <note>original: *salut des ames*</note> that have been committed to them. ### Retreat of the Pastors of France.

...can be done by some individuals or some families, and what can be done by an entire great body of Churches, composed of several Flocks <note>original: *Troupeaux*; the local congregations of believers.</note> dispersed throughout almost all the Provinces of a great Kingdom. They saw the cloud that threatened them with a great storm gathering every day; however, they could not protect themselves from it, nor shelter themselves in any manner; because they were either too far from places of retreat, or too entangled in the danger to be able to leave it. I want no other witnesses than the Pastors <note>original: *Pasteurs*; the Protestant ministers.</note> themselves; they can say better than anyone if one can justly reproach their Flocks by saying that they were warned of the danger threatening them, that they were able to escape it, and that they preferred to remain exposed to all these dangers due to temporal considerations <note>Meaning worldly concerns, such as property, wealth, or social status, rather than spiritual ones.</note>, rather than withdrawing to places where they could have preserved the advantage of serving God in peace. 

For my part, I am convinced that if, in driving the Pastors out of France, the persecutors had permitted them to take with them all those who would have wanted to accompany them in their retreat, there would not have been one who would not have brought with him the largest part of his Flock; and I dare say, based on the knowledge I have of what occurred in this regard, that even if the reasoning I have just refuted were generally as good and just as it is in fact flawed, one could not legitimately apply it specifically to the Churches of France, nor derive any advantage from it for the Pastors who abandoned them through the retreat for which our Author here makes his Apology <note>A formal written defense, likely referring to a specific contemporary text justifying the ministers' flight during the persecutions following the Revocation of the Edict of Nantes.</note>.

## Disinterested Sentiments on the...

...holy impulses, as an example that shows Pastors <note>The term "Pastor" here refers to the ministers of the French Reformed (Huguenot) Church during the period of state-sponsored persecution under Louis XIV.</note> what their affection for their Flocks should be, and which shows that the salvation of the souls who have been committed to them must be, at the very least, as dear to them as their own. It seems to me that one can well reason thus on these examples: Moses asks God to be blotted out of His book if He wants to make His people of Israel perish; and St. Paul wished to be separated from Christ for his brothers. All the more reason these two illustrious servants of God would have chosen to expose themselves for this people to all the torments that persecutors use against the faithful, without fearing the danger that their individual salvation might have run there.

Secondly, although people still dispute what Moses and St. Paul had in mind when they expressed themselves thus; it is agreed upon in this: that their charity moved them to sacrifice their own interests to those of the preservation or the salvation of the people for whom one wanted to be *blotted out of the book* <note>A reference to Exodus 32:32, where Moses offers his own soul to save the Israelites from divine wrath.</note>, and the other *separated from Jesus Christ* <note>A reference to Romans 9:3, where Paul expresses a willingness to be "accursed" for the sake of his kin.</note>. No more is needed to be able to draw a favorable conclusion here. 

Finally, although it was a matter of neither persecution in the one nor the other of these examples, it does not follow that one cannot very legitimately use them as a precedent; since they wanted to make—in whatever manner one explains the terms they used—a sacrifice, beyond comparison, much greater than all those a Pastor can make for his Flock in the time of the most horrible persecution. <meta>Continuing from page 209, the text continues its critique of the historical examples used to justify the flight of pastors during persecution. # Retreat of the Pastors of France.
<meta>catchword: cal-</meta>

...truth is often distorted. These are, above all, the people of the world whose sentiments should be the most suspect in everything concerning the glory of those they take for their Heroes. Just as they make no scruple of exaggerating their good qualities to speak of marvelous things, they also do not feel much conscience in giving praise even to their faults, in order to make them pass for more perfect than they are or have been. **Gregory of Nyssa** <note>A 4th-century theologian and one of the "Cappadocian Fathers."</note> has visibly fallen into all these faults while writing the **Panegyric** <term>A formal public speech of high praise, which in this era often prioritized rhetorical flair and moral inspiration over strict historical accuracy.</term> of **Gregory of Neocaesarea**. He speaks wonders of him everywhere, but wonders so extraordinary and so poorly chosen that one would have to blind oneself to avoid seeing that they are nothing but crude fables.

I place in this category what he says concerning the apparition of the **Blessed Virgin** and **Saint John the Evangelist**; it is a tale worthy of being inserted into the *Legends* <note>Likely a reference to hagiographic collections like the *Golden Legend*, which the author views with skepticism.</note>, but very little worthy of being believed by people of good sense. I also place there the miracle of the lake dried up in a single night by his prayers to reconcile two brothers who were disputing its possession, and the "judicious" comparison that his Panegyrist makes between this judgment and that of **Solomon** <note>A reference to the biblical account of King Solomon’s wisdom in deciding between two women claiming the same baby (1 Kings 3).</note> when he settled the dispute between the two mothers over a child. I further place there the miracle of the staff planted in the ground which became a tree that the waters of the **Lycus River** <note>A river in the Roman province of Pontus, in modern-day Turkey.</note> dared not approach during its floods. But I place above all that of the Demons cast out of a Temple of Idols. This is what happened:

<margin>*Gregory to Satan, enter.* <note>original: "Gregorius Satane, intra."</note></margin>
He was called so <note>Referring to the name "Thaumaturgus," which means "Wonder-worker."</note> because of the gift he had for performing miracles. Having entered one day into a Temple of Idols to take shelter from the rain, and being obliged to spend the night there, he first drove out the Demons by the invocation of the name of J. C. <note>Jesus Christ.</note> and by the sign of the cross which he made in the air. But the Priest of the Idol, having begged him to make the Demons return to the Temple, Gregory—out of <term>condescension</term> <note>In this context, it refers to a yielding or gracious compliance, though the author uses it here with a tone of disbelief.</note> for this Priest—gave him a scrap from a book, on which he had written these words: **Gregory to Satan, enter**. The Priest, having received this note, placed it upon the Altar, and after performing his usual incantations, he saw his Demons return as was their custom. In all honesty, can one tell stories more crudely than by telling tales of this nature? And if it were true that Gregory Thaumaturgus, after having driven the Demons from a Temple, had recalled them there, and had thereby given occasion for the abominable incantations of an idolatrous Priest, is it not true that he would have deserved much more blame than praise? It is, however, **Gregory of Nyssa** who tells all these fine tales in this *Life of Gregory Thaumaturgus*, which the Author of the *Apology* regards as a History; and it is for this reason that, for my part, I cannot give any credit either to the facts he relates there, or to the judgments he passes on those facts, because I find neither any verisimilitude in most of these facts, nor any justice in these sorts of judgments.

However, I would not wish to deny that **Gregory Thaumaturgus** performed several miracles in his time, since the name has remained with him; I believe, as Socrates <note>Socrates Scholasticus (c. 380 – after 439 AD), a Greek Christian church historian and contemporary of Sozomen and Theodoret.</note> [re-...]

It was in this way that St. Anianus acted, as appears in the sacred Histories. The Blessed Lupus <note>Loup of Troyes, famous for protecting his city from Attila the Hun.</note> did the same, as is sung in his Hymn. And it was in this way that St. Remi, Bishop of Reims, did not abandon his Church when the Franks, who were Pagans, came into Belgica <note>The Roman province of Gallia Belgica, roughly modern-day northern France and Belgium.</note> (which was a Province of his Diocese), but he tamed the ferocity of that nation through his prayers and his holy examples, and appeased those monsters by his words.*

One may very well relate here the example of those Bishops of Spain of whom St. Augustine speaks in this same letter to Honoratus: *There were some, he says, who fled after their people had been either dispersed or scattered; but there were many more who remained exposed to the same dangers, with the people who had stayed behind.* These were undoubtedly the examples that our Pastors should have followed. # Retreat of the Pastors of France.

...ples <note>Completing the word "Disciples" from the previous page.</note>, which was undoubtedly a flight and a desertion very contrary to the rules of their duty; and consequently very unworthy of the approval of their Divine Master. Indeed, one need only consider the state in which Jesus Christ found himself then to see clearly that there was never an occasion or circumstance where it would have been less permitted—or to put it better, more forbidden—to flee than it was then for the Disciples of the Son of God by the laws of their duty. There were several false witnesses who accused their Divine Master, who charged him with several crimes of which he was innocent, and for which, nevertheless, he was going to be punished with an infamous execution. His Disciples had been witnesses to his life and his conversation <note>In this 17th-century context, "conversation" refers to one’s social conduct and way of life.</note>, and they could have, by their true testimony, destroyed all these false accusations and all these calumnies. Were they not obliged by the laws of duty to bear the testimony they owed to innocence, and to make every effort to prevent it from being condemned and oppressed? They undoubtedly were; these are duties of justice and equity that the light of reason can make known to all men; and besides, there is an express law in chapter 5 of Leviticus which obliges those who have knowledge of something, whether by having seen it or by having known it, to declare it in judgment under penalty of bearing their <margin>*Lev. 5. 1.*</margin> iniquity <note>Original: "porter son iniquité," a biblical phrase meaning to bear the guilt or punishment for a sin.</note>. If this law had to be strictly observed when it was a matter of charging the guilty, so that crimes would not remain unpunished, for even greater reason should it be observed when it was a matter of exonerating the

<meta>The previous page discussed different viewpoints or "sentiments" regarding religious conduct. This page continues that discussion by citing historical church authorities on the ethics of fleeing during times of persecution.</meta>

# Disinterested Sentiments on the...

...very reasonable sentiments. Here is how [he] explained himself regarding the passage in question:
"Therefore, when they persecute you," etc. <note>Matthew 10:23: "When they persecute you in one town, flee to the next."</note>
"It is not," says Bede, <note>Bede the Venerable (c. 672–735), an English monk and influential early medieval scholar.</note> "that they should dread sufferings, but rather they ought to avoid them so that the occasion of their afflictions might serve the advancement of the Gospel; [ensuring] that the preaching of the faith is not cut short by the death of the preachers, but on the contrary, that it might be spread elsewhere by their flight. It is thus that by fleeing, they could care for the advancement of the Gospel not only in other places, but even in the places from which they had fled, whether during their absence or upon their return. This flight was therefore their victory; for they fled not out of a motive of fear, but out of a motive of love for Jesus Christ."

It is asked whether this is a precept <term>precept|A divine command that must be followed</term> or merely a permission? I respond: it is in part a precept, such as when the necessity of the Church and of the faith, or the danger of succumbing [to temptation], requires that one takes flight; for, as [John] Chrysostom <note>Saint John Chrysostom (c. 347–407), an important Early Church Father known for his powerful preaching.</note> says, he who flees for fear of denying Jesus Christ does not renounce Him by that flight. It is in part a counsel, <term>counsel|In theology, a 'counsel of perfection' is a recommendation for spiritual growth that is not strictly mandatory for all.</term> such as when one can expect a greater advantage through flight, or a greater perfection for oneself or for others. It is in part a permission, such as when someone greatly fears the torments and is not constrained to remain in the places where they are being persecuted, neither by necessity nor by obligation—as they would be, for example, if they were...

<summary>This page explores the theological justification for fleeing religious persecution, citing Bede and John Chrysostom to argue that flight can be a strategic "victory" for the Gospel rather than an act of cowardice.</summary>
<keywords>persecution, flight, Bede the Venerable, John Chrysostom, martyrdom, Gospel advancement, religious law, Christian ethics</keywords>

->Retreat of the Pastors of France.<-

...the work of their <term>calling<note>vocation: a divine call to religious service</note></term> as soon as they are persecuted, so that they persevere until the end.

I say that the same thing appears when considering this passage in relation to the circumstances of the time and the particular Ministry with which Jesus Christ <note>original: "J.C."</note> had then just charged the Disciples to whom he gave this order. For these circumstances show that, by all appearances, the <term>persecutions</term> they were to endure during the course of this first Ministry were not to be of the kind one might call "persecutions to the utmost" <note>original: "persecutions à toute outrance" — meaning extreme, total, or life-ending persecution</note>; and that consequently, it was more natural and more necessary to give them an order to oblige them to continue the work of their Ministry, without being discouraged by these minor persecutions, than to grant them permission to flee as a remedy or as a means of putting themselves entirely under cover. 

In fact, there was not yet any political law at that time forbidding the preaching that the Kingdom of Heaven was at hand; on the contrary, all those to whom this great news was to be announced were in expectation of this Kingdom, because the time marked by the <term>Prophets</term> had been, or was about to be, fulfilled. If there was anything that could cause the Disciples of Jesus Christ to be persecuted, it was only the humble appearance of their persons, which in no way agreed with the ideas that most of the Jews had of this Kingdom. They considered it an earthly and temporal Kingdom, and believed that its Ministers should appear with a worldly brilliance and magnificence that corresponded to the ideas they had of their Messiah and of his Kingdom. But all this could not

Be that as it may, it is certain that this passage—whether considered in itself, or in relation to the circumstances of the time, or in relation to the specific Ministry with which Jesus Christ <note>original: "Jesus-Christ"</note> had just then charged his twelve Disciples, or in relation to the words that precede, or in relation to those that immediately follow—can only be considered as an order by which he wished to oblige his Disciples to persevere constantly in the work of their Ministry, without being turned away or discouraged by the persecutions they would have to suffer from men. So that one may say with much reason, that this passage, far from being favorable, and far from being able to serve to justify the conduct of our Pastors, evidently condemns it; for they have abandoned the work of their Ministry, and the first persecutions they endured discouraged them from the dangers and persecutions to which they ought to have either exposed themselves or remained exposed for the salvation of their Flocks.

<meta>This page continues the theological debate regarding the duties of a "Good Pastor" (Shepherd) and when it is permissible for a minister to flee persecution versus when they must face death for their flock. It references the Gospel of John and the Gospel of Matthew.</meta>

->Sentiments of the disinterested on the<-
<meta>catchword: He</meta>

Every good Pastor must lay down his life for his sheep; not at all times and imprudently, but when God wills it and manifests His will to him with sufficient evidence. The reason he gives for this is taken from the necessity of explaining this passage in a manner that does not find it contradictory to the permission that Jesus Christ gives to flee <margin>Luke 15.</margin> sometimes. The second is taken from the description that Jesus Christ makes in another place of a <margin>P. 263.</margin> good Pastor, where He shows him in distress over a stray sheep and exposing himself to the danger of losing his life for it: which is not quite the same as laying down his life for his sheep. And the third, finally, is taken from the three characteristics which constitute, he says, the <term>hireling<note>Original: "mercenaire"; a term from John 10:12 referring to a worker who cares for the sheep only for pay and abandons them in danger, unlike the true shepherd.</note></term>; and upon which he makes several considerations.

I have already shown in advance that there is no contradiction between the passage in question here, and that of the 10th chapter of St. Matthew, and that consequently it is in no way necessary to have recourse to the restriction in question to reconcile one with the other. Thus, to refute the first response of our Author, one need only consider the restriction in itself, to see if it must be admitted, and if, being admitted, it can destroy the objection for which he wants it to serve as an answer. Every good Pastor, he says, must lay down his life for his sheep not at all times and imprudently, but when God wills it and manifests His will to him with sufficient evidence. This proposition can undoubtedly have a good sense and be found very true: but it is also certain that in this sense it does not give the slightest blow to the objection in question.

<summary>The author debates the conditions under which a pastor must sacrifice his life for his congregation, attempting to reconcile the biblical command to be a "Good Shepherd" with the practical permission to flee persecution.</summary>

-># Disinterested Sentiments on the<-

duty, which only obligate him to procure the advancement of the glory of God and the salvation of one's neighbor <note>original: "prochain"</note> as much as is possible for him.

But also, when a Pastor is in none of these cases; when the persecution afflicting the Church is a general persecution; when the persecutors carry their cruelty to its ultimate excesses without the presence or absence of the Pastors affecting its increase or decrease; when his Church persists, whether its members have remained standing or have succumbed through weakness; when there are many faithful who ardently desire his presence and the continuation of the work of his Ministry; and when the Pastor himself is in a position to continue his services to them, it is certain that he is obliged to do so. There is no danger, however evident it may be; nor persecution, however violent it may be; nor bad faith and treachery from the persecutors, nor any other thing of this nature, that can exempt him from it, nor authorize him to abandon his Flock <note>The capitalization of "Troupeau" (Flock) emphasizes the sacred relationship between the minister and the congregation.</note> and take flight. This is because such flight and desertion would be manifestly contrary to the laws of his duty; to the engagements he has with his Church; and to the particular obligation he has to work with all his power for the advancement of the glory of God and the salvation of the souls entrusted to him, for which he is obliged to give an account.

This being so, as it seems to me I have sufficiently proved, it only remains to examine whether the Pastors of France <note>This likely refers to the Huguenot pastors during the reign of Louis XIV, particularly around the time of the Revocation of the Edict of Nantes (1685), when Protestant ministers were given the choice of conversion or exile.</note> were in any of these cases; whether their presence could no longer serve for any purpose in the advancement of the

### Withdrawal of the Pastors of France

...horrible storm, for whose salvation it was necessary to be continually in action. And, as no one is held to the impossible, one may say that God himself had, in a sense, broken the bonds of their commitment by bringing them to this great age or by afflicting them with these infirmities. So much so that their presence could no longer serve anything, neither the advancement of God’s glory nor the edification of the Church; they should neither expose themselves nor remain exposed to the dangers of persecution. Their <term>withdrawal<note>French: "retraite." In this context, it refers to the "Refuge"—the flight of Huguenot ministers to Protestant countries like England, Holland, or Prussia.</note></term> cannot thus be charged with any blame; on the contrary, it would have been a matter of rashness to remain in the state in which they found themselves: for since they could no longer work for the salvation of others, they were obliged to put their own salvation in safety, and to preserve by their withdrawal *the nobility of their faith*.

It is not quite the same for those of the second Class—I mean those whose Churches had been forbidden <note>The French "interdit" refers to the legal "interdiction" or forced closure of Protestant temples by the royal authorities, a tactic used to dismantle the Huguenot infrastructure before the final Revocation.</note> before the revocation of the <term>Edict<note>The Edict of Nantes (1598), which had granted French Protestants limited religious freedom until it was revoked by Louis XIV in 1685.</note></term> and the general banishment of all the Pastors. For although they have not quite deserved the same blame as those of the fourth <insert>class</insert>, it is certain nonetheless that they are not entirely exempt from it, and that there is always something to be said regarding their withdrawal. They can only excuse it by saying that at the time they left their Churches, there were still others remaining which had Pastors, and which their <term>flocks<note>Their congregations.</note></term> could join to be edified and consoled; and that, being forbidden to remain in the places where the <term>exercise<note>French: "l'exercice." This refers specifically to the "exercise of religion," the formal public practice of Reformed worship.</note></term> had been prohibited, they

# Disinterested Sentiments on the...

...were reduced to the necessity of seeking other establishments elsewhere, in order to continue the work of their <term>Ministry<note>French: "Ministere." Refers to the office and duties of a Protestant pastor.</note></term>. But one may also answer them that it would have been much more appropriate had they remained close to their <term>Flocks<note>French: "Troupeaux." A common biblical metaphor for the congregation of a church.</note></term>, and that, no longer being able to assemble them publicly in the accustomed places, they should have assembled them from time to time and secretly in the most convenient places they could have found; in order to maintain there the purity of the faith, and to animate their zeal for the preservation of this precious deposit. One may further answer them that if men had forbidden them from approaching the places where their Flocks were accustomed to assemble, God commanded them quite the opposite, and that *it is better to obey God than men* <note>original: "il vaut mieux obeïr à Dieu qu'aux hommes." A reference to Acts 5:29, a key scriptural justification used by Huguenots to resist royal decrees that contradicted their religious conscience.</note>. God had given them as Pastors to these afflicted Flocks, and they were charged with the accounting of their souls. He himself had formed the bonds of their engagement, and these were bonds that the authority of men could not and should never break. It was a thousand times better to expose oneself to all sorts of dangers than to submit to these unjust orders which cannot, consequently, be regarded as legitimate excuses for their <term>withdrawal<note>French: "retraite." In this context, it refers to the pastors' flight from France to seek refuge in Protestant countries like Holland or England.</note></term>.

It is true that those who would have wanted to take the side I am describing, and hold secret assemblies in the places where the exercise [of religion] had been forbidden, might have encountered several obstacles there, even from the members of their own Churches.
